(b)

For each
i\xrightarrow{a}j\xrightarrow{b}k
in
I
the following is commutative:

\textstyle{F(i)}
\textstyle{F(j)X(a)}
\textstyle{F(k)X(ba)}
\textstyle{F(k)X(b)X(a).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi(a)}
\scriptstyle{F(k)\,X_{b,a}}
\scriptstyle{\psi(ba)}
\scriptstyle{\psi(b)X(a)}

Definition 4.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
and
(F,\psi)\colon X\to\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
be in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then

(1)

(F,\psi)
is called an
I
-
precovering
(of
{\mathscr{C}}
) if
for any
i,j\in I_{0}
,
x\in X(i),y\in X(j)
,
the morphism

(F,\psi)_{x,y}^{(1)}\colon\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)\to{\mathscr{C}}(F(i)x,F(j)y)

of
\Bbbk
-complexes
defined by the following is an isomorphism:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}F(j)}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}{\mathscr{C}}(F(j)X(a)x,F(j)y)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}{\mathscr{C}}(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y)}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}{\mathscr{C}}(F(i)x,F(j)y)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{summation}}{\mathscr{C}}(F(i)x,F(j)y),

the precise form of which is given as follows:

\displaystyle(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{x,y}(((f^{n}_{a})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\psi(a)_{x}*F(j)(f_{a})

(4.5)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}F(j)(f_{a})^{n-r}\circ\psi(a)_{x}^{r}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}F(j)(f_{a})^{n}\circ\psi(a)_{x}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}},

where the second term is computed by using (
3.3
), and
the last term uses the fact that
\psi(a)_{x}
is concentrated in degree 0 (Remark
3.11
).

(2)

(F,\psi)
is called an
I
-
covering
if it is an
I
-precovering and is
dense
,
i.e., for each
c\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
there exists an
i\in I_{0}
and
x\in X(i)_{0}

such that
F(i)(x)
is isomorphic to
c
in
{\mathscr{C}}
.

5.
Grothendieck constructions

In this section we define a 2-functor
\textstyle\int\colon\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})\to\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
whose
correspondence on objects is a
\mathbb{V}
-enriched version of (the opposite version of)
the original Grothendieck construction (cf.
[
47
]
).
In particular, we deal with the case of
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
later.

Definition 5.1
.

We define a
2
-functor
\textstyle\int\colon\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})\to\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
,
which is called the
Grothendieck construction
.

On objects.
Let
X=(X(i),X_{i},X_{b,a})\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})_{0}
.
Then
\textstyle\int X\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}_{0}
is defined as follows.

•

(\textstyle\int X)_{0}:=\bigcup_{i\in I_{0}}\{i\}\times X(i)_{0}=\{{}_{i}x:=(i,x)\mid i\in I_{0},x\in X(i)_{0}\}
.

•

For each
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
, we set

(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y):=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y).

•

For any
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y,{}_{k}z\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
and
each
f=(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)
,

g=(g_{b})_{b\in I(j,k)}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{j}y,{}_{k}z)
, we set

g\circ f:=\left(\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}a\,\in\,I(i,j)\\
b\,\in\,I(j,k)\\
c\,=\,ba\end{smallmatrix}}g_{b}\circ X(b)f_{a}\circ X_{b,a}x\right)_{c\,\in\,I(i,k)},

which is the composite of the following:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int X)({}_{j}y,{}_{k}z)\times(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{k}z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b\in I(j,k)}X(k)(X(b)y,z)\times\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{c\in I(i,k)}X(k)(X(c)x,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}\{X(k)(X(b)y,z)\times X(j)(X(a)x,y)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}X(k)(X(ba)x,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}\{X(k)(X(b)y,z)\times X(j)(X(b)X(a)x,X(b)y)\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}X(k)(X(b)X(a)x,z),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}(1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}\times X(b))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b,a}X(k)(X_{b,a}x,\,z)}
summation

(5.6)

where elements are mapped as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((g_{b})_{b},(f_{a})_{a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\sum_{c=ba}g_{b}\circ X(b)f_{a}\circ X_{b,a}x\right)_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{b},f_{a})_{b,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{b}\circ X(b)f_{a}\circ X_{b,a}x)_{b,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{b},X(b)f_{a})_{b,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{b}\circ X(b)f_{a})_{b,a}.}

Note here that the composition with
X_{b,a}x
is “contravariant”,
which is used in (
5.8
).

•

For each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
the identity
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}x}
is given by

1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}x}=(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}X_{i}\,x)_{a\in I(i,i)}\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}X(i)(X(a)x,x),

where
\delta
is the Kronecker delta
1
1

1

This is used to mean that the
a
-th component is
\eta_{i}\,x
if
a=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}
,
or 0 otherwise.

.

On 1-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})
and
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{b,a}^{\prime})
be objects of
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
, and let

(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a 1-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
Then a 1-morphism

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon\textstyle\int X\to\textstyle\int X^{\prime}

in
\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
is defined as follows.

•

For each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
,
\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x):={}_{i}(F(i)x)
.

•

Let
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.
Then we define

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)\to(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}(F(i)x),{}_{j}(F(j)y))

as the composite

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}F(j)}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X^{\prime}(j)(F(j)X(a)x,F(j)y)

(5.7)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X^{\prime}(j)(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y)}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)x,F(j)y).

Namely, for each
f=(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)
,
we set

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)(f):=(F(j)f_{a}\circ\psi(a)x)_{a\in I(i,j)}.

On 2-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})
and
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{b,a}^{\prime})
be objects of
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
,

(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
and
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime\prime}
1-morphisms in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
,
and let
\zeta\colon(F,\psi)\Rightarrow(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
be a 2-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
Then a 2-morphism

\textstyle\int\zeta\colon\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\Rightarrow\textstyle\int(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})

in
\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
is defined by

(\textstyle\int\zeta){}_{i}x:=\begin{cases}\zeta(i)_{x}\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(F(i)x)&\text{\ if\ }a=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}\\
0&\text{\ if\ }a\neq 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}\end{cases}

in
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
for each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.

In particular, in the case that
\mathbb{V}={\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk)
, i.e., that

\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
,
the precise form of the
Grothendieck construction

\textstyle\int\colon\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}

is described as follows.

On objects.
Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}
.
Then
\textstyle\int X\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
is defined as follows.

•

(\textstyle\int X)_{0}:=\bigcup_{i\in I_{0}}\{i\}\times X(i)_{0}=\{{}_{i}x:=(i,x)\mid i\in I_{0},x\in X(i)_{0}\}
.

•

For each
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
, we set

(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y):=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X(j)^{n}(X(a)x,y),

where note that
X(j)(X(a)x,y)
is a dg
\Bbbk
-module.

•

For any
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y,{}_{k}z\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
and
each
f=(f^{p}_{a})_{a\in I(i,j),p\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)
,

g=(g^{q}_{b})_{b\in I(j,k),q\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{j}y,{}_{k}z)
, it turns out that

\displaystyle g\circ f

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}a\,\in\,I(i,j)\\
b\,\in\,I(j,k)\\
c\,=\,ba\end{smallmatrix}}X_{b,a}x*(g_{b}\circ(X(b)f_{a}))\right)_{c\,\in\,I(i,k),n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}a\,\in\,I(i,j)\\
b\,\in\,I(j,k)\\
c\,=\,ba\end{smallmatrix}}\sum_{p,r\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}g^{n-r-p}_{b}\circ(X(b)f_{a})^{p}\circ(X_{b,a}x)^{r}\right)_{c\,\in\,I(i,k),n\in\mathbb{Z}}

because of the contravariant part in (
5.6
).

•

For each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
the identity
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}x}
is given by

1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}x}=(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}X_{i}\,x)_{a\in I(i,i)}\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}X(i)(X(a)x,x)=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}X(i)^{p}(X(a)x,x).

On 1-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})
and
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{b,a}^{\prime})
be objects of
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and let

(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a 1-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then a 1-morphism

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon\textstyle\int X\to\textstyle\int(X^{\prime})

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
is defined as follows.

•

For each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
,
\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x):={}_{i}(F(i)x)
.

•

Let
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.
Then we define

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)\to(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}(F(i)x),{}_{j}(F(j)y))

as in (
5.7
).
Namely, for each
f=((f^{n}_{a})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)\linebreak(X(a)x,y)
, we have

\displaystyle((f^{n}_{a})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle\mapsto((F(j)(f^{n}_{a}))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi(a)_{x}*((F(j))(f^{n}_{a}))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=((F(j)(f_{a})^{n}\circ\psi(a)_{x})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}\quad\text{(cf.\ \eqref{eq:opp-comp})}

Thus we have

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)(f)=((F(j)(f_{a})^{n}\circ\psi(a)_{x})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)}.

(5.9)

On 2-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})
and
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},X_{i}^{\prime},X_{b,a}^{\prime})
be objects of
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,

(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
and
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon X\to X^{\prime}
1-morphisms in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
and let
\zeta\colon(F,\psi)\Rightarrow(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
a 2-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then a 2-morphism

(\textstyle\int\zeta)\colon\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\Rightarrow\textstyle\int(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
is defined by

(\textstyle\int\zeta)({}_{i}x)=\begin{cases}\zeta(i)_{x}\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(F(i)x)=(\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\zeta(i)^{n-r}_{x}\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(F(i)x)^{r})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{\ if\ }a=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}\\
0&\text{\ if\ }a\neq 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}\end{cases}

in
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
for each
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.

Example 5.2
.

Let
A
be a dg
\Bbbk
-algebra with the differential
d_{A}

regarded as a dg
\Bbbk
-category with a single object.
Then
A\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
.
Consider the functor
X:=\Delta(A)\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
.
Then it is straightforward to verify the following.

(1)

If
I
is a free category defined by the quiver
1\to 2
,
then
\textstyle\int X
is isomorphic to the triangular dg algebra

\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
A&A\end{bmatrix}
.

(2)

If
I
is a free category
{\mathbb{P}}Q
defined by a quiver
Q
,
then
\textstyle\int X
is isomorphic to the dg path-category
AQ
of
Q
over
A

defined as follows:

•

(AQ)_{0}:=Q_{0}
.

•

For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
,

AQ(i,j):=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}Q(i,j)}A\mu=\left\{\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}Q(i,j)}a_{\mu}\mu\,\right|\,\left.(a_{\mu})_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}\in\bigoplus_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}A\right\}.

•

For any
i,j,k\in Q_{0}
, the composition

AQ(j,k)\times AQ(i,j)\to AQ(i,k)
is given by

\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(j,k)}b_{\nu}\nu\times\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}a_{\mu}\mu\mapsto\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j),\\
\nu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(j,k)\end{smallmatrix}}b_{\nu}a_{\mu}\nu\mu=\sum_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,k)}\left(\sum_{\lambda=\nu\mu}b_{\nu}a_{\mu}\right)\lambda.

•

For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set
(AQ)^{n}(i,j)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}A^{n}\mu
.

•

For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
the differential
d:(AQ)^{n}(i,j)\to(AQ)^{n+1}(i,j)
is given by

d\left(\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}a_{\mu}\mu\right)=\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}d_{A}(a_{\mu})\mu,

which automatically satisfies the graded Leibniz rule.

Indeed, we can define an isomorphism
\phi\colon AQ\to\textstyle\int X
as follows:
We regard
A
as a category with a single objects
*
.
Then for each
i\in Q_{0}
, we have
X(i)_{0}=\{*\}
and
X(i)_{1}=A
.
Then
(\textstyle\int X)_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i\in Q_{0}}X(i)_{0}=\bigcup_{i\in Q_{0}}\{{}_{i}*\}=\{{}_{i}*\mid i\in Q_{0}\}
.
Therefore, we define a bijection
\phi_{0}\colon(AQ)_{0}\to(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
by
i\mapsto{}_{i}*
.
For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
,
since we have
(AQ)(i,j)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}A\mu
,
and

(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}*,{}_{j}*):=\bigoplus_{\mu\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(\mu)*,*)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in I(i,j)}X(j)_{1}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q(i,j)}A,

we define a bijection
\phi_{1}\colon(AQ)(i,j)\to(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}*,{}_{j}*)
by

\sum_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q}a_{\mu}\mu\mapsto(a_{\mu})_{\mu\in{\mathbb{P}}Q}
.
Then
\phi:=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\colon AQ\to\textstyle\int X
turns out to be an isomorphism.

(3)

If
I
is a poset
S
,
then
\textstyle\int X
is isomorphic to the incidence dg category
AS
of
S
over
A

defined as follows:

•

(AS)_{0}:=S
as a set.

•

For any
i,j\in S
,
(AS)(i,j):=\begin{cases}A&\text{if $i\leq j$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

For any
i,j,k\in S
, the composition
AS(j,k)\times AS(i,j)\to AS(i,k)

is given by the multiplication of
A
for the case that
i\leq j\leq k
,
and as zero otherwise.

•

For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set

(AS)^{n}(i,j):=\begin{cases}A^{n}&\text{if $i\leq j$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

•

For any
i,j\in Q_{0}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
the differential
d:(AS)^{n}(i,j)\to(AS)^{n+1}(i,j)
is given by

d_{A}\colon A^{n}\to A^{n+1}
if
i\leq j
, and as zero otherwise,
which automatically satisfies the graded Leibniz rule.

Indeed, we can define an isomorphism
\phi\colon AS\to\textstyle\int X
as follows:
We regard
A
as a category with a single objects
*
.
Then for each
i\in S
, we have
X(i)_{0}=\{*\}
and
X(i)_{1}=A
.
Then
(\textstyle\int X)_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{0}}X(i)_{0}=\bigcup_{i\in I_{0}}\{{}_{i}*\}=\{{}_{i}*\mid i\in S\}
.
Therefore, we define a bijection
\phi_{0}\colon(AS)_{0}\to\textstyle\int(X)_{0}
by
i\mapsto{}_{i}*
.
For any
i,j\in S
,
since we have
(AS)(i,j)=\begin{cases}A&\text{if $i\leq j$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
,
and

(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}*,{}_{j}*):=\bigoplus_{\mu\in S(i,j)}X(j)(X(\mu)*,*)=\bigoplus_{\mu\in S(i,j)}X(j)_{1}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in S(i,j)}A=A,\text{if $i\leq j$},

we define a bijection
\phi_{1}\colon(AS)(i,j)\to\textstyle\int(X)({}_{i}*,{}_{j}*)
by

\sum_{\mu\in S}a_{\mu}\mu\mapsto(a_{\mu})_{\mu\in S}
.
Then
\phi:=(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\colon AQ\to\textstyle\int X
turns out to be an isomorphism.

(4)

If
I
is a monoid
G
,
then
\textstyle\int X
is isomorphic to the monoid dg algebra
2
2

2

Since
AG
has the identity
1_{A}1_{G}
,
this is regarded as a category with a single object.

AG
of
G
over
A
defined as follows:

•

AG:=\bigoplus_{g\in G}Ag
.

•

The multiplication
AG\times AG\to AG
is defined by

\left(\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}g\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{h\in G}b_{h}h\right):=\sum_{g,h\in G}(a_{g}b_{h})gh=\sum_{f\in G}\left(\sum_{gh=f}a_{g}b_{h}\right)f.

•

For each
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(AG)^{n}:=\bigoplus_{g\in G}A^{n}g
.

•

The differential
d:(AG)^{n}\to(AG)^{n+1}
is given by

d\left(\sum_{g\in G}a_{g}g\right):=\sum_{g\in G}d_{A}(a_{g})g
,
which automatically satisfies the graded Leibniz rule.

In (3) above,
AS
is defined to be the factor category
of the dg path-category
AQ
modulo the ideal
generated by the full commutativity relations in
Q
,
where
Q
is the Hasse diagram of
S
regarded as a quiver by
drawing an arrow
x\to y
if
x\leq y
in
Q
.
If
S
is a finite poset, then
AS
is identified with the usual incidence dg algebra.

See
[
9
]
for further examples of the Grothendieck constructions
of functors, further examples of the Grothendieck constructions
of a functor
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
will be done in the forthcoming paper.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
We define a left transformation
(P_{X},\phi_{X}):=(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)

(called the
canonical morphism
) as follows.

•

For each
i\in I_{0}
, the functor
P(i)\colon X(i)\to\textstyle\int X
is defined by

\left\{\begin{aligned} P(i)x&:={}_{i}x\\
P(i)f&:=(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f\circ(X_{i}\,x))_{a\in I(i,i)}\colon{}_{i}x\to{}_{i}y\text{\ in\ $\textstyle\int(X)$}\end{aligned}\right.

for all
f\colon x\to y
in
X(i)
.

•

For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, the natural transformation

\phi(a)\colon P(i)\Rightarrow P(j)X(a)

\textstyle{X(i)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int(X)}
\textstyle{X(j)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P(i)}
\scriptstyle{P(j)}
\scriptstyle{X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\phi(a)}

is defined by
\phi(a)x:=(\delta_{b,a}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})_{b\in I(i,j)}
for all
x\in X(i)_{0}
.

Now let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
The left transformation
(P_{X},\phi_{X}):=(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)
is as follows.

•

For each
i\in I_{0}
, the dg functor
P(i)\colon X(i)\to\textstyle\int(X)
is defined by
P(i)x:={}_{i}x
for all
x\in X(i)_{0}
, and
by setting
P(i)f\colon{}_{i}x\to{}_{i}y
as

\displaystyle P(i)f:

\displaystyle=(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}(X_{i}\,x)*f)_{a\in I(i,i)}

(5.10)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}f^{n-r}\circ(X_{i}\,x)^{r}\right)_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\right)_{a\in I(i,i)}

for all
f\colon x\to y
in
X(i)
.
Note here that the map

{\mathscr{C}}(X_{i}(x),y)\colon{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)\to{\mathscr{C}}(X(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})x,y)
,
f\mapsto f\circ X_{i}x

is given by the contravariant functor
{\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},y)
at
X_{i}x
.

•

For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, the dg natural transformation

\phi(a)\colon P(i)\Rightarrow P(j)X(a)

\textstyle{X(i)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int X}
\textstyle{X(j)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P(i)}
\scriptstyle{P(j)}
\scriptstyle{X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\phi(a)}

is defined by
\phi(a)x:=(\delta_{b,a}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})_{b\in I(i,j)}
for all
x\in X(i)_{0}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The
(P,\phi)
defined above is a
1
-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.

Proof.

This is straightforward by using Remark
4.2
.
∎

Consider the cases that

X
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat})
and that
X
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
In both cases, we have the canonical
I
-covering

(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)
as shown below.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat})_{0}
.
Then the canonical morphism
(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)

is an
I
-covering.
More precisely, the morphism

(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}\colon\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)\to(\textstyle\int X)(P(i)x,P(j)y)

is the identity
for all
i,j\in I_{0}
and all
x\in X(i)_{0}
,
y\in X(j)_{0}
.

Proof.

By the definitions of
\textstyle\int(X)_{0}
and of
P
it is obvious
that
(P,\phi)
is dense.
Let
i,j\in I_{0}
and
x\in X(i)
,
y\in X(j)
.
We only have to show that

(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}\colon\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)\to(\textstyle\int X)(P(i)x,P(j)y)

is the identity.
Let
f=(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)
.
Then by noting the form of
f_{a}\colon X(a)x\to y
in
X(j)
,
we have the following equalities for each
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

by (
4.5
), (
5.10
) and (
5.8
):

\displaystyle(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}(f)^{n}=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}P(j)(f_{a})^{n}\circ\phi(a)_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}\sum_{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-s)s}f_{a}^{n-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\right)_{b\in I(j,j)}\circ\phi(a)_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}\sum_{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-s)s}f_{a}^{n-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\right)_{b\in I(j,j)}\circ(\delta_{c,a}1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})_{c\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\hskip-5.0pt\left(\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}b\in I(j,j)\\
c\in I(i,j)\\
d=bc\end{smallmatrix}}\hskip-10.0pt\delta_{b,1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}(-1)^{(n-r-s)s}f_{a}^{n-r-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\circ X(b)(\delta_{c,a}1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})^{0}\circ(X_{b,c}x)^{r}\hskip-5.0pt\right)_{\hskip-5.0pt{d\in I(i,j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}\sum_{r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}(-1)^{(n-r-s)s}f_{a}^{n-r-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\circ X(1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j})(1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})^{0}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{r}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}\sum_{r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}(-1)^{(n-r-s)s}f_{a}^{n-r-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{r}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}r,s,t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\\
n=r+s+t\end{smallmatrix}}(-1)^{rs+rt+st}f_{a}^{t}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{r}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}\quad(m:=r+s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}r,m,t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\\
n=m+t\end{smallmatrix}}(-1)^{r(m-r)+mt}f_{a}^{t}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{(m-r)}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{r}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}m,t\in{\mathbb{Z}}\\
n=m+t\end{smallmatrix}}(-1)^{mt}f_{a}^{t}\circ\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(m-r)r}X_{j}(X(a)x)^{(m-r)}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{r}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}((X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x*X_{j}(X(a)x)))*f_{a})^{n}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}\hskip 155.00023pt

\displaystyle\overset{*}{=}\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x}*f_{a})^{n}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}=f^{n}.

In the above the equality
\overset{*}{=}
holds.
Indeed, let
(\operatorname{-})^{\mathrm{op}}\colon X(j)\to X(j)^{\mathrm{op}}
be the
canonical contaravariant functor defined by

u^{\mathrm{op}}:=u
for all
u\in X(j)_{0}\cup X(j)_{1}
, and
(h\circ g)^{\mathrm{op}}=g*h
for all morphisms
g\colon u\to v,h\colon v\to w
in
X(j)
.
If we have an equality
h\circ g=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{u}
in
X(j)
,
then we have
g*h=(h\circ g)^{\mathrm{op}}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{u}^{\mathrm{op}}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{u}
.
By applying this fact to the case that

g=X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x,h=X_{j}(X(a)x),u=X(a)x
, we have

X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x*X_{j}(X(a)x)=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x}
.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}
.
Then the canonical morphism
(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)

is an
I
-covering.
More precisely, the morphism

(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}\colon\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)\to(\textstyle\int X)(P(i)x,P(j)y)

is the identity
for all
i,j\in I_{0}
and all
x\in X(i)_{0}
,
y\in X(j)_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost the same.
The difference is that

X_{j}
and
X_{b,c}
are dg natural transformations, and thus their degrees are
0
.
This makes the long computation above simpler as follows.

\displaystyle(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}(f)^{n}=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}P(j)(f_{a})^{n}\circ\phi(a)_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}\sum_{s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-s)s}f_{a}^{n-s}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)^{s}\right)_{b\in I(j,j)}\circ\phi(a)_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}f_{a}^{n}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)\right)_{b\in I(j,j)}\circ(\delta_{c,a}1\kern-2.31248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})_{c\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\hskip-5.0pt\left(\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{\begin{smallmatrix}b\in I(j,j)\\
c\in I(i,j)\\
d=bc\end{smallmatrix}}\hskip-10.0pt\delta_{b,1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}}\hskip-5.0pt\sum_{r\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(-1)^{(n-r)r}f_{a}^{n-r}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)\circ X(b)(\delta_{c,a}1\kern-2.31248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})^{0}\circ(X_{b,c}x)^{r}\hskip-5.0pt\right)_{\hskip-5.0pt{d\in I(i,j)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}f_{a}^{n}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)\circ X(1\kern-2.31248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j})(1\kern-2.31248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})^{0}\circ(X_{1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)^{0}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}f_{a}^{n}\circ X_{j}(X(a)x)\circ(X_{1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x)\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}f_{a}^{n}\circ(X_{j}(X(a)x)\circ X_{1\kern-2.01248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x))\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}\hskip 143.37405pt

\displaystyle\overset{*}{=}\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\left(\delta_{d,a}f_{a}^{n}\circ 1\kern-2.31248pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x}\right)_{d\in I(i,j)}=f^{n}.

The equality
\overset{*}{=}
holds since

X_{j}(X(a)x)\circ X_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j},a}x=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x}
.

∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}
and

H\colon\textstyle\int X\to{\mathscr{C}}
be in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
and consider the composite
1
-morphism

(F,\psi)\colon X\xrightarrow{(P,\phi)}\Delta(\textstyle\int X)\xrightarrow{\Delta(H)}\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
.
Then
(F,\psi)
is an
I
-covering if and only if
H
is an equivalence.

Proof.

Obviously
(F,\psi)
is dense if and only if so is
H
.
Further for each
i,j\in I_{0}
,
x\in X(i)
and
y\in X(j)
,

(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{x,y}
is an isomorphism if and only if so is
H_{{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y}

because
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}(F(i)x,F(j)y)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int(X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{x,y}}
\scriptstyle{(P,\phi)^{(1)}_{x,y}}
\scriptstyle{H_{{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y}}

by Proposition
5.6
.
∎

6.
Adjoints

In this section we will show that the Grothendieck construction is a strict left adjoint to
the diagonal 2-functor, and that
I
-coverings are essentially given
by the unit of the adjunction.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
.
We define a functor
Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\colon\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{C}}
by

•

Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}({}_{i}x):=x
for all
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))_{0}
; and

•

Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}((f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}):=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}f_{a}

for all
(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in(\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)

and for all
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))_{0}
.

It is easy to verify that
Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}
is a
\mathbb{V}
-functor.

Theorem 6.2
.

The
2
-functor
\textstyle\int\colon\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})\to\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
is a strict left
2
-adjoint to
the
2
-functor
\Delta\colon\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}\to\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
The unit is given by the family
of canonical morphisms
(P_{X},\phi_{X})\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)

indexed by
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
, and the counit is given by the family of

Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\colon\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{C}}

defined as above indexed by
{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
.

In particular,
(P_{X},\phi_{X})
has a strict universality
in the comma category
(X\!\!\downarrow\!\!\Delta)
,
i.e., for each
(F,\psi)\colon X\to\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
with
{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
,
there exists a unique
H\colon\textstyle\int X)\to{\mathscr{C}}
in
\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
such that
the following is a commutative diagram in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
:

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\Delta({\mathscr{C}}).}
\textstyle{\Delta(\textstyle\int X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{(P_{X},\phi_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(H)}

Proof.

For simplicity set
\eta:=((P_{X},\phi_{X}))_{X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})_{0}}
and

\varepsilon:=(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}})_{{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}_{0}}
.

Claim 1
.

\Delta\varepsilon\cdot\eta\Delta=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Delta}
.

Indeed,
let
{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
.
It is enough to show that

\Delta(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}})\cdot(P_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})},\phi_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})})=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}
.
Now

\displaystyle\mathrm{LHS}

\displaystyle=\left((Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}P_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(i))_{i\in I_{0}},(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\phi_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(a))_{a\in I_{1}}\right),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHS}

\displaystyle=\left((1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}})_{i\in I_{0}},(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}}})_{a\in I_{1}}\right).

First entry
\colon
Let
i\in I
.
Then
Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}P_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(i)=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}}

because for each
x,y\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
and each
f\in{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)
we have

(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}P_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(i))(x)=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}({}_{i}x)=x
; and

(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}P_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(i))(f)=(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f\cdot((\eta_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})})_{i}\,x))_{a\in I_{1}}=\sum_{a\in I(i,i)}\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f=f
.

Second entry
\colon
Let
a\colon i\to j
in
I
.
Then
Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\phi_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(a)=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}}}
because
for each
x\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
we have

Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\left(\phi_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}(a)x\right)=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\left((\delta_{b,a}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})(a)x})_{b\in I(i,j)}\right)=\sum_{b\in I(i,j)}\delta_{b,a}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{x}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{x}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}}x}
.
This shows that
\mathrm{LHS}=\mathrm{RHS}
.

Claim 2
.

\varepsilon\textstyle\int\cdot\textstyle\int\eta=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\textstyle\int}
.

Indeed, let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
It is enough to show that

Q_{\textstyle\int X}\cdot\textstyle\int(P_{X},\phi_{X})=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\textstyle\int X}
.

On objects
\colon
Let
{}_{i}x\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.
Then
Q_{\textstyle\int X}\left(\textstyle\int(P_{X},\phi_{X})(x)\right)=Q_{\textstyle\int X}({}_{i}(P_{X}(i)x))={}_{i}x
.

On morphisms
\colon
Let
f=(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\colon{}_{i}x\to{}_{j}y
be in
\textstyle\int(X)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle Q_{\textstyle\int X}\textstyle\int(P_{X},\phi_{X})(f)=Q_{\textstyle\int X}((P_{X}(j)(f_{a})\circ\phi_{X}(a)x)_{a\in I(i,j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}P_{X}(j)(f_{a})\circ\phi_{X}(a)x=(P_{X},\phi_{X})^{(1)}_{x,y}(f)=f.

Thus the claim holds.
The two claims above prove the assertion.
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
(F,\psi)\colon X\to\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
be in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat})
.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

(F,\psi)
is an
I
-covering;

(2)

There exists an equivalence
H\colon\textstyle\int X\to{\mathscr{C}}
such that
the diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\Delta({\mathscr{C}})}
\textstyle{\Delta(\textstyle\int(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{(P_{X},\phi_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(H)}

is strictly commutative.

Proof.

This immediately follows by Theorem
6.2
and Lemma
5.7
. More precisely,

\displaystyle(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{x,y}(((f^{n}_{a})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})_{a\in I(i,j)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\psi(a)_{x}*F(j)(f_{a})

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}H\phi(a)_{x}*HP(j)(f_{a})

\displaystyle=H(\sum_{a\in I(i,j)}\phi(a)_{x}*P(j)(f_{a}))

\displaystyle=H(P,\phi)_{x,y}^{(1)}(f).

∎

In particular, in the case that
\mathbb{V}={\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk)
, i.e.,

\mathbb{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
, we have the following.

The
2
-functor
\textstyle\int\colon\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
is a strict left
2
-adjoint to
the
2
-functor
\Delta\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
The unit is given by the family
of canonical morphisms
(P_{X},\phi_{X})\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)

indexed by
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and the counit is given by the family of

Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\colon\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{C}}

defined as above indexed by
{\mathscr{C}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
.

In particular,
(P_{X},\phi_{X})
has a strict universality in the comma category

(X\!\!\downarrow\!\!\Delta)
, i.e., for each
(F,\psi)\colon X\to\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
with
{\mathscr{C}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
,
there exists a unique
H\colon\textstyle\int(X)\to{\mathscr{C}}
in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
such that
the following is a commutative diagram in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
:

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\Delta({\mathscr{C}}).}
\textstyle{\Delta(\textstyle\int X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{(P_{X},\phi_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(H)}

7.
The derived colax functors

Let
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
be a colax functor.
In this section we formulate
the definition of
the “derived category
{\mathscr{D}}(X)
” of
X

as a colax functor from
I
to a
2
-category of triangulated categories
by modifying the definition given in the previous paper

[
7
]
.
We first recall the definition of colax functors between
2
-categories.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}
be 2-categories.

(1) A
colax functor
from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
is a triple

(X,\eta,\theta)
of data:

•

a triple
X=(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2})
of maps
X_{i}\colon\mathbf{B}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}_{i}
(
\mathbf{B}_{i}
denotes the
collection of
i
-morphisms of
\mathbf{B}
for each
i=0,1,2
) preserving domains and codomains of all 1-morphisms and 2-morphisms
(i.e.
X_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{1}(i,j))\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{1}(X_{0}i,X_{0}j)

for all
i,j\in\mathbf{B}_{0}
and
X_{2}(\mathbf{B}_{2}(a,b))\subseteq\mathbf{C}_{2}(X_{1}a,X_{1}b)

for all
a,b\in\mathbf{B}_{1}
(we omit the subscripts of
X
below));

•

a family
\eta:=(\eta_{i})_{i\in\mathbf{B}_{0}}
of 2-morphisms
\eta_{i}\colon X(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(i)}
in
\mathbf{C}

indexed by
i\in\mathbf{B}_{0}
; and

•

a family
\theta:=(\theta_{b,a})_{(b,a)}
of 2-morphisms

\theta_{b,a}\colon X(ba)\Rightarrow X(b)X(a)

in
\mathbf{C}
indexed by
(b,a)\in\operatorname{com}(\mathbf{B}):=\{(b,a)\in\mathbf{B}_{1}\times\mathbf{B}_{1}\mid ba\text{ is defined}\}

satisfying the axioms:

(i)

(X_{1},X_{2})\colon\mathbf{B}(i,j)\to\mathbf{C}(X_{0}i,X_{0}j)
is a functor
for all
i,j\in\mathbf{B}_{0}
;

(ii)

For each
a\colon i\to j
in
\mathbf{B}_{1}
the following are commutative:

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.6201pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.6201pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise (2) A
lax functor
from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
is a colax functor
from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\text{co}}
(see Notation
2.17
).

(3) A
pseudofunctor
from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
is a colax functor with
all
\eta_{i}
and
\theta_{b,a}
2-isomorphisms.

(4) We define a 2-category
\operatorname{Colax}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})
having all the colax functors

\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{C}
as the objects as follows.

1-morphisms.

(b)

For each
i\xrightarrow{a}j\xrightarrow{b}k
in
\mathbf{B}_{1}
the following is commutative:

\textstyle{X^{\prime}(ba)F(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(b)X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(b)F(j)X(a)}
\textstyle{F(k)X(ba)}
\textstyle{F(k)X(b)X(a).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{X^{\prime}(b)\psi(a)}

\scriptstyle{\psi(ba)}
\scriptstyle{\psi(b)X(a)}

2-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,\eta,\theta)
,
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})

be colax functors from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
, and

(F,\psi)
,
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
1-morphisms from
X
to
X^{\prime}
.
A
2
-morphism
from
(F,\psi)
to
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
is a
family
\zeta=(\zeta(i))_{i\in\mathbf{B}_{0}}
of 2-morphisms

\zeta(i)\colon F(i)\Rightarrow F^{\prime}(i)
in
\mathbf{C}

indexed by
i\in\mathbf{B}_{0}

such that the following is commutative for all
a\colon i\to j
in
\mathbf{B}_{1}
:

\textstyle{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(a)F^{\prime}(i)}
\textstyle{F(j)X(a)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(j)X(a).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X^{\prime}(a)\zeta(i)}
\scriptstyle{\zeta(j)X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\psi(a)}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}(a)}

Composition of 1-morphisms.

Let
X=(X,\eta,\theta)
,
X^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
and
X^{\prime\prime}=(X^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})

be colax functors from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
, and
let
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
,
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\colon X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime\prime}

be 1-morphisms.
Then the composite
(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})(F,\psi)
of
(F,\psi)
and

(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
is a 1-morphism from
X
to
X^{\prime\prime}
defined by

(F^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})(F,\psi):=(F^{\prime}F,\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi),

where
F^{\prime}F:=((F^{\prime}(i)F(i))_{i\in\mathbf{B}_{0}}
and for each
a\colon i\to j
in
\mathbf{B}
,

(\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)(a):=F^{\prime}(j)\psi(a)\circ\psi^{\prime}(a)F(i)

is the pasting of the diagram

\textstyle{X(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}(i)}
\textstyle{X(j)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(j)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}(j).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X(a)}
\scriptstyle{X^{\prime}(a)}
\scriptstyle{F(i)}
\scriptstyle{F(j)}
\scriptstyle{\psi(a)}
\scriptstyle{X^{\prime\prime}(a)}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(i)}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}(a)}

Remark 7.2
.

We make the following remarks.

(1)

Note that a (strict) 2-functor from
\mathbf{B}
to
\mathbf{C}
is a pseudofunctor with
all
\eta_{i}
and
\theta_{b,a}
identities.

(2)

By regarding the category
I
as a 2-category with all 2-morphisms identities,
the definition (1) of colax functors above coincides
with Definition
2.12
.

(3)

When
\mathbf{B}=I
, the definition (4) of
\operatorname{Colax}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})

above coincides with that of
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbf{C})
given before.

(4)

It is well-known that the composite of pseudofunctors turns out to be a pseudofunctor.

Notation 7.3
.

We introduce the following
2
-categories.

(1)

\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{AB}
denotes the
2
-category of light abelian
\Bbbk
-categories,

\Bbbk
-functors between them,
and natural transformations between these
\Bbbk
-functors.

(2)

\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}
denotes the
2
-category of light Frobenius
\Bbbk
-categories,

\Bbbk
-functors between them,
and natural transformations between these
\Bbbk
-functors.

(3)

\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}
denotes the
2
-category of light triangulated
\Bbbk
-categories,
triangle
\Bbbk
-functors between them,
and natural transformations between these triangle
\Bbbk
-functors.

(4)

\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
denotes the
2
-category of
2
-moderate triangulated
\Bbbk
-categories,
triangle
\Bbbk
-functors between them,
and natural transformations between these triangle
\Bbbk
-functors.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}_{0}
.
A dg functor
{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)
is called a
right dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-module
.
We set

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})

\displaystyle:=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}({\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)),\text{and}

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

\displaystyle:=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}({\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)).

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})
is called the
category of
(
right
)
dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-modules
, and

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
is called the
dg category of
(
right
)
dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-modules
.
Thus in particular, we have

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0},

which consists of the right dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-modules.
Note that
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})
is in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{AB}
, or more presicely, in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}
,
whereas
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
is in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT}
and in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCAT}
.
By (
3.4
),
they have the following relation for all objects
X,Y
:

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y)=Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y)).

Thus a morphism
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})
is given as a dg natural transformation, but in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
it is given as a derived transformation.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be small dg categories.

(1)

A dg functor
{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)
is called a
left dg
{\mathscr{B}}
-module
.
A
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodule is a dg functor
M\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)
.

(2)

For each
B\in{\mathscr{B}}_{0}
and
A\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
, we set
{}_{B}M:=M(\operatorname{-},B)\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)

and
M_{A}:=M(A,\operatorname{-})\colon{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)
, and
{}_{B}M_{A}:=M(A,B)
.
Note that
{}_{B}M
is a right dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-module,
M_{A}
is a left dg
{\mathscr{B}}
-module,
and
{}_{B}M_{A}
is a dg
\Bbbk
-module.

(3)

If
f\colon A^{\prime}\to A
is a morphism in
{\mathscr{A}}
, and
g\colon B^{\prime}\to B
is a morphism in
{\mathscr{B}}
,
then we set
{}_{g}M:=M(\operatorname{-},g)
and
M_{f}:=M(f,\operatorname{-})
.
Note that
M_{f}\colon M_{A}\to M_{A^{\prime}}
is a derived transformation between dg functors,
and
that
{}_{g}M\colon{}_{B^{\prime}}M\to{}_{B}M
is a derived transformation between dg functors.

(4)

To emphasise that
M
is a
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodule, we sometimes use the notation

{}_{\mathscr{B}}M_{\mathscr{A}}
.

(5)

The dg category
{\mathscr{A}}
defines an
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodule

{\mathscr{A}}(\operatorname{-},?)\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)

by
(x,y)\mapsto{\mathscr{A}}(x,y)
.
We denote this bimodule by
{}_{\mathscr{A}}{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathscr{A}}
,
and we use the same convention that

{}_{x}{\mathscr{A}}:={\mathscr{A}}(\operatorname{-},x)
,
{\mathscr{A}}_{y}:={\mathscr{A}}(y,\operatorname{-})
, and

{}_{x}{\mathscr{A}}_{y}:={\mathscr{A}}(y,x)
for all
x,y\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
; and
for any morpisms
f\colon x^{\prime}\to x
,
g\colon y^{\prime}\to y
in
{\mathscr{A}}
,
we write

{}_{g}{\mathscr{A}}\colon{}_{y^{\prime}}{\mathscr{A}}\to{}_{y}{\mathscr{A}}
, and

{\mathscr{A}}_{f}\colon{\mathscr{A}}_{x}\to{\mathscr{A}}_{x^{\prime}}
.
Sometimes we abbreviate them as
g^{\wedge}\colon{y^{\prime}}^{\wedge}\to y^{\wedge}
and

{}^{\wedge}f\colon{}^{\wedge}x\to{}^{\wedge}x^{\prime}
, respectively.

Remark 7.6
.

In Definition
7.5
(3),
note that
0
-cocycle morphisms are preserved by the correspondences

f\mapsto M_{f}
and
g\mapsto{}_{g}M
, namely,
if
f\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{A}}(A^{\prime},A))
(resp.
g\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{B}}(B^{\prime},B))
),
then
M_{f}\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})(M_{A},M_{A^{\prime}}))={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})(M_{A},M_{A^{\prime}})

(resp.
{}_{g}M\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

({}_{B^{\prime}}M,{}_{B}M))={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})({}_{B^{\prime}}M,{}_{B}M)
).

Indeed,
since
M_{A}
is a left dg
{\mathscr{B}}
-module,
the dg functor
M\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)

induces a dg functor
M:{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})
.
Therefore, for any
A,A^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
, by noting that
{\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}}(A,A^{\prime})={\mathscr{A}}(A^{\prime},A)
,
it induces a chain map

M_{A,A^{\prime}}:{\mathscr{A}}(A^{\prime},A)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})(M_{A},M_{A^{\prime}}).

Hence if
f\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{A}}(A^{\prime},A))
, then

M_{f}\in Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})(M_{A},M_{A^{\prime}}))
.
Thus in this case,

M_{f}\colon M_{A}\to M_{A^{\prime}}

is a dg natural transformation between dg functors.
Similar argument works for the remaining case.

Notation 7.7
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime},{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}
be small dg categories,

E\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{A}}
,
F\colon{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{B}}
dg functors, and

M
an
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule.

(1)

We denote by
{}_{E}M
,
M_{F}
and
{}_{E}M_{F}

the
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule,
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}
-bimodule, and
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}
-bimodule
defined as follows, respectively:

\displaystyle{}_{E}M

\displaystyle:=M(\operatorname{-},E(?))=M\circ(1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\otimes_{k}E)\colon{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\otimes_{k}E}{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{M}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk),

\displaystyle M_{F}

\displaystyle:=M(F(\operatorname{-}),?)=M\circ(F\otimes_{k}1\kern-2.12503pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{A}}})\colon{{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{F\otimes_{k}1\kern-2.06113pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{A}}}}{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{M}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk),\text{and}

\displaystyle{}_{E}M_{F}

\displaystyle:=M(F(\operatorname{-}),E(?))=M\circ(F\otimes_{k}E)\colon{{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F\otimes_{k}E}{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{M}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk).

(2)

Moreover, if
E^{\prime}\colon{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{A}}
and
F^{\prime}\colon{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{B}}
are dg functors,
and
\alpha\colon E\Rightarrow E^{\prime}
,
\beta\colon F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
are derived transformations,
then
M
defines morphisms of bimodules as follows:

\displaystyle{}_{\alpha}M

\displaystyle:=M(\operatorname{-},\alpha_{(?)})=M\circ(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\otimes_{k}\alpha)\colon{}_{E}M\to{}_{E^{\prime}}M

\displaystyle M_{\beta}

\displaystyle:=M(\beta_{(\operatorname{-})},?)=M\circ(\beta\otimes_{k}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{A}}})\colon M_{F^{\prime}}\to M_{F},\text{and}

\displaystyle{}_{\alpha}M_{\beta}

\displaystyle:=M(\beta_{(\operatorname{-})},\alpha_{(?)})=M\circ(\beta\otimes_{\Bbbk}\alpha)\colon{}_{E}M_{F^{\prime}}\to{}_{E^{\prime}}M_{F}.

(3)

We often abbreviate the morphism of
{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodules
(induced from the bimodule
{}_{{\mathscr{A}}}{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathscr{A}}
)

{}_{\alpha}{\mathscr{A}}\colon{}_{E}{\mathscr{A}}\to{}_{E^{\prime}}{\mathscr{A}}\quad\text{as}\quad\overline{\alpha}\colon\overline{E}\to\overline{E^{\prime}},

and the morphism of
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}^{\prime}
-bimodules (induced from the bimodule
{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{\mathscr{B}}
)

{\mathscr{B}}_{\beta}\colon{\mathscr{B}}_{F^{\prime}}\to{\mathscr{B}}_{F}\quad\text{as}\quad\overline{\beta}^{*}\colon\overline{F^{\prime}}^{*}\to\overline{F}^{*}.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{C}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}
be small dg categories, and

{}_{\mathscr{D}}W_{\mathscr{C}},{}_{\mathscr{C}}V_{\mathscr{B}}
,
{}_{\mathscr{B}}U_{\mathscr{A}}
bimodules.
Then the canonical isomorphism

\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{a}_{W,V,U}\colon W\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}(V\otimes_{\mathscr{B}}U)\to(W\otimes_{\mathscr{C}}V)\otimes_{\mathscr{B}}U

that represent the associativity of the tensor products
is called the
associator
of tensor products.

Definition 7.9
.

Since both
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
and
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}
are 2-categories,
the correspondences
{\mathscr{A}}\mapsto{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

and
{\mathscr{A}}\mapsto\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})

are extended to representable 2-functors

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}^{\prime}:=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}((\operatorname{-})^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)))

\displaystyle\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCAT}^{\mathrm{coop}}\text{ and}

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}:=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}((\operatorname{-})^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)))

\displaystyle\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}^{\mathrm{coop}},

respectively.
By modifying these, we define pseudofunctors

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}

\displaystyle\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCAT}\text{ and}

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}

as follows.
For any diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}
(resp. in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
),
we define
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(E):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{E}{\mathscr{B}}
and

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\alpha):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{\alpha}{\mathscr{B}}

(resp.
{\mathscr{C}}(E):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{E}{\mathscr{B}}
and

{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{\alpha}{\mathscr{B}}
) (see Notation
7.7
).
Note that

{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{\alpha}{\mathscr{B}}
is defined by
regarding
\alpha
in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
as a
2
-morphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}
concentrated in degree 0.

We define the structures of pseudofunctors for
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}
as follows.

•

For each
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}_{0}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
, we define

\eta_{\mathscr{A}}\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathscr{A}})\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})}

(resp.
\eta_{\mathscr{A}}\colon{\mathscr{C}}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathscr{A}})\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})}
)
by setting

\eta_{\mathscr{A}}M\colon M\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{\mathscr{A}}(?,\operatorname{-})\to M

to be the canonical isomorphisms for all
M\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
.

•

For each pair of dg functors
{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{F}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{G}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}_{1}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{1}
,
we define

\displaystyle\theta_{G,F}

\displaystyle\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(GF)\Rightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(G)\circ{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(F)

\displaystyle(\text{resp.}\ \theta_{G,F}

\displaystyle\colon{\mathscr{C}}(GF)\Rightarrow{\mathscr{C}}(G)\circ{\mathscr{C}}(F))

as the canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{GF}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}\Rightarrow(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{F}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime})\otimes_{{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}}{}_{G}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}

given as the composite of the canonical isomorphisms
(see Definition
7.8
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{}_{GF}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}({\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}(?,F(\operatorname{-}))\otimes_{{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}(?,G(\operatorname{-})))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{a}_{(\operatorname{-}),{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}(?,F(\operatorname{-})),{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}(?,G(\operatorname{-}))}^{-1}}(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}(?,F(\operatorname{-})))\otimes_{{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime}}{\mathscr{A}}^{\prime\prime}(?,G(\operatorname{-})).

It is straightforward to check that this defines pseudofunctors
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}
.

Remark 7.10
.

In the definition above, note that
{\mathscr{C}}(E)
is a left adjoint to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(E)

and that
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(E)
has also a right adjoint.
Therefore,
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(E)
is an exact functor.

Remark 7.11
.

Using the notation in Definition
7.5
(5),
the Yoneda embedding
Y_{\mathscr{A}}\colon{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

can be defined
as the functor sending a morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
{\mathscr{A}}
to
the morphism
f^{\wedge}\colon x^{\wedge}\to y^{\wedge}
in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

Definition 7.12
.

As is easily seen,
the stable category construction
{\mathscr{F}}\mapsto\underline{{\mathscr{F}}}
can be
extended to a
2
-functor
\mathrm{st}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}

in an obvious way.
Then we set

{\mathscr{H}}:=\mathrm{st}\circ{\mathscr{C}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI},

which turns out to be a pseudofunctor
as a composite of a pseudofunctor and a 2-functor.
For each
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
,

{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
is called the
homotopy category
of
{\mathscr{A}}
.

For each
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}_{0}
, we set

{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}}):={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})[\mathrm{qis}^{-1}]

to be the quotient category of the homotopy cagtegory of
{\mathscr{A}}
with respect to quasi-isomorphisms, and call it the
derived category
of
{\mathscr{A}}
.

Remark 7.13
.

We note that for each
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}
,
the homotopy category
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
is a light triangulated category, but
the derived category
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
is a properly 2-moderate triangulated category
although there exist isomorphisms
(
7.14
) below.

Definition 7.14
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}_{0}
.

(1)

We denote by
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})

the full subcategory of the homotopy category
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
of
{\mathscr{A}}
consisting of
the
homotopically projective
objects
M
, i.e., objects
M
such that
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(M,A)=0

for all acyclic objects
A
.

(2)

Let
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}}{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{Q_{{\mathscr{A}}}}{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})

be the inclusion functor and the quotient functor, respectively,
and set
\mathbf{j}_{{\mathscr{A}}}:=Q_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}
.
Then there exists a functor
\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})

giving a left adjoint
\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}
to
Q_{\mathscr{A}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{{\mathscr{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

such that

\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbf{j}_{{\mathscr{A}}}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})}

(7.13)

is satisfied
3
3

3

This can be done by taking
\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}(P):=P
for all
P\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

and the counit

\varepsilon_{{\mathscr{A}}}\colon(\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}})\circ Q_{\mathscr{A}}\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})}

consists of quasi-isomorphisms

\varepsilon_{{\mathscr{A}},M}\colon\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}M\to M
for all
M\in{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
.
In particular, both
\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}
and
\mathbf{j}_{{\mathscr{A}}}
are equivalences and
quasi-inverses to each other, and by the adjoint above we have a canonical
isomorphism

{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})(L,M)\cong{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}(L),M)

(7.14)

for all
L,M\in{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
.
Here, note that the right hand sides are always small, but the left hand sides are not.

Definition 7.15
.

We define two
2
-categories
{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
and
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
as follows:

•

{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}:=\{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}\}
.

•

For any objects
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}}),{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
of
{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,

1
-morphisms from
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})
to
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
are the
\Bbbk
-functors

F\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})

satisfying the condition

F(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0})\subseteq\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}

(7.15)

When this is the case, we say that
F

preserves homotomically projectives
.

•

{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}:=\{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}\}
.

•

For any objects
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}}),{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
of
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
),

1
-morphisms from
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
to
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
are
the
\Bbbk
-functors of the form
\underline{F}\,(:=\mathrm{st}(F))

for some
\Bbbk
-functors
F\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})

satisfying the condition (
7.15
).

•

In both
2
-categories, the
2
-morphisms are the natural transformations between those
1
-morphisms.

Remark 7.16
.

(1) By definition, the
2
-functor
\mathrm{st}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}

restricts to a
2
-functor

\mathrm{st}\colon{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}).

(2) In the definition above, unlike objects,
note that we defined the
1
-morphisms in
{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

not as the “image” of
1
-morphisms in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
under
{\mathscr{C}}
.

Nevertheless, pseudofunctors

{\mathscr{C}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}\ \text{and}\ {\mathscr{H}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}

restrict to pseudofunctors

{\mathscr{C}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\ \text{and}\ {\mathscr{H}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}).

Indeed,
let
F\colon{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{B}}
be a
1
-morphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
.
It is enough to show that

{\mathscr{C}}(F)
is a
1
-morphism in
{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, i.e. that

{\mathscr{C}}(F)(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0})\subseteq\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}
.
Let
P\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
.
Then since
{\mathscr{C}}(F)
is a left adjoint to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(F)
, we have

{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})({\mathscr{C}}(F)(P),A)\cong{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(P,{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(F)(A))

for all acyclic objects
A
of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
The right hand side is zero because
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(F)
is exact by Remark
7.10
, and hence
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}(F)(A)
is acyclic in
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
This shows that
{\mathscr{C}}(F)(P)\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}
.
Noting that
{\mathscr{C}}(F)=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{A}}}{}_{F}{\mathscr{B}}
and
that
{}_{A}({}_{F}{\mathscr{B}})={\mathscr{B}}(\operatorname{-},F(A))
is a projective
{\mathscr{B}}
-module
for all
A\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
,
this argument is generalized in Lemma
7.18
below.

Since we would like to make the domain of a pseudofunctor
\mathbf{L}
wider,
we adapted this definition of
1
-morphisms.

Definition 7.17
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be small dg categories.
A
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodule
U
is said to be
right homotopically projective

if
{}_{B}U
is a homotopically projective right dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-module for all
B\in{\mathscr{B}}_{0}
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
U
be a
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodule for dg categories
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

U
is right homotopically projective.

(2)

The dg functor
\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}}U\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

preserves homotopically projective objects.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).
For any
P\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}
and any acyclic object
A\in{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
,
taking
H^{0}
to the isomorphism

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(P\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}}U,A)\cong{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})(P,{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(U,A))
, we have

{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(P\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}}U,A)\cong{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})(P,{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(U,A)).

The right hand side is
0

because for each
B\in{\mathscr{B}}_{0}
,
({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(U,A))(B)
becomes acyclic, which is shown by

H^{i}(({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(U,A))(B))=H^{i}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})({}_{B}U,A))={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})({}_{B}U,A[i])=0

for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Hence
P\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}}U\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).
Let
B\in{\mathscr{B}}_{0}
.
Then by (2),
{}_{B}U\cong{}_{B}{\mathscr{B}}\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}}U
is homotopically projective
because so is
{}_{B}{\mathscr{B}}
.
∎

.

\mathbf{L}

{\mathscr{D}}

To define
\mathbf{L}
, consider a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

in
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
We set
\mathbf{L}({\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})):={\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
Since
E(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0})\subseteq\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}
by definition of
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,

E
restricts to a functor
E|\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})
,
and we can define
\mathbf{L}(E)
as the
composite
\mathbf{L}(E):=\mathbf{j}_{\mathscr{B}}\circ E|\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}
as in the diagram

Moreover, using the restriction
\alpha|\colon E|\Rightarrow F|
of
\alpha
,
we define
\mathbf{L}(\alpha)
by setting

\mathbf{L}(\alpha):=\mathbf{j}_{\mathscr{B}}\circ\alpha|\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}
.

Then for any functors
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{E}{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})\xrightarrow{E^{\prime}}{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{C}})

in
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, we have

\mathbf{L}(E^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(E)=\mathbf{L}(E^{\prime}\circ E).

(7.16)

Indeed, since
\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{B}}\circ\mathbf{j}_{\mathscr{B}}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})}
(see (
7.13
)), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(E^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(E)

\displaystyle=j_{\mathscr{C}}\circ E^{\prime}|\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{B}}\circ\mathbf{j}_{\mathscr{B}}\circ E|\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}

\displaystyle=j_{\mathscr{C}}\circ E^{\prime}|\circ E|\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}=\mathbf{L}(E^{\prime}\circ E).

Also, for each
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\in{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, we have a natural isomorphism

\eta_{\mathscr{A}}\colon 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}\Longrightarrow Q_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}=Q_{\mathscr{A}}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})}\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}=\mathbf{L}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})})

given by the unit of the adjoint
\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}\dashv Q_{\mathscr{A}}
.
These natural isomorphisms define a structure of a pseudofunctor for
\mathbf{L}
,
and it is easy to check that
\mathbf{L}
is in fact a pseudofunctor.
Finally, we define
{\mathscr{D}}

as the composite

{\mathscr{D}}:=\mathbf{L}\circ{\mathscr{H}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2},

which is a pseudofunctor as the composite of pseudofunctors.

Since
\mathbf{L}
is a pseudofunctor satisfying
(
7.16
), we have the following.
This will be used in the proof of Theorem
9.17
.

Lemma 7.20
.

The pseudofunctor
\mathbf{L}\colon{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
strictly preserves
the vertical and the horizontal compositions of
2
-morphisms.
More precisely, let
E,F,G\colon{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
,

E^{\prime},F^{\prime}\colon{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})\to{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{C}})
,
and
\alpha\colon E\Rightarrow F
,
\beta\colon F\Rightarrow G,\gamma\colon E^{\prime}\Rightarrow F^{\prime}

be in
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(\beta\bullet\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}(\beta)\bullet\mathbf{L}(\alpha),\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(\gamma\circ\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}(\gamma)\circ\mathbf{L}(\alpha).

Proof.

Straightforward by (
7.13
).
∎

Definition 7.21
.

(1) Define a
2
-subcategory
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
of
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT}
as follows:
Objects are the dg categories of the form
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
for
some
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
,
1
-morphisms are the dg functors

F\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})
preserving homotopically projective objects
with
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
,
and
2
-morphisms are the dg natural transformations between these
1
-morphisms.

(2) By noting the fact that
for any
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y)

\displaystyle=Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y))

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y)

\displaystyle=H^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(X,Y))

for all objects
X,Y\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
, we define

2
-functors
Z^{0}
and
H^{0}
in the
the following diagram so that it turns out to be
strictly commutative:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

For each
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}
,

Z^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})):={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})
and
H^{0}({\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}}))

:={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
Next, let
E,F\colon

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})
be dg functors in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

and
\alpha\colon E\Rightarrow F
a dg natural transformation.
We define
Z^{0}(E),Z^{0}(\alpha)
and
H^{0}(E),H^{0}(\alpha)
as follows.
We set

(Z^{0}(E))(M):=E(M),\text{and }(H^{0}(E))(M):=E(M)

for all
M\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}={\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
.
For each
M,N\in{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
, the dg functor

E
induces a chain map

E(M,N)\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(M,N)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})(E(M),E(N)).

Using this we set

\displaystyle(Z^{0}(E))(M,N)

\displaystyle:=Z^{0}(E(M,N))\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})(M,N)\to{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})(E(M),E(N)),\text{and}

\displaystyle(H^{0}(E))(M,N)

\displaystyle:=H^{0}(E(M,N))\colon{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})(M,N)\to{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})(E(M),E(N)).

Finally, by noting that
\alpha_{M}\in{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})(E(M),F(M))
, we set

\displaystyle(Z^{0}(\alpha))_{M}

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{M}\in{\mathscr{C}}(E(M),F(M)),\text{and}

\displaystyle(H^{0}(\alpha))_{M}

\displaystyle:=H^{0}(\alpha_{M})\in{\mathscr{H}}(E(M),F(M))

Note here that if
\alpha
above were just a general derived transformation, then neither
Z^{0}(\alpha)
nor
H^{0}(\alpha)
is defined.
This is a reason why we consider colax functors
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
rather than

X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCat}
below.

Remark 7.22
.

Consider a dg functor
F\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})

with
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
.
Here, we do not assume the condition
F(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0})\subseteq\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})_{0}
.
Even in this case,
F
induces a triangle functor

H^{0}(F):{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
, and it is possible to define

\mathbf{L}(H^{0}(F))\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})

by

\mathbf{L}(H^{0}(F)):=Q_{\mathscr{B}}\circ H^{0}(F)\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}
as in the left part of the diagram

}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}}}
