{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 117.9 36.86 M 117.9 36.86 C 132.28 11.96 132.28 -11.96 118.18 -36.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.5}{-0.86603}{0.86603}{-0.5}{85.30827pt}{-26.46713pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{95.35274pt}{-1.71112pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

although
H^{0}(F)
may not in the domain of the pseudofunctor
\mathbf{L}
.

Of course, if
F
preserves homotopically projective objects, then these two definitions of
\mathbf{L}(H^{0}(F))

coincide because
we have the restriction
H^{0}F|\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{B}})
satisfying the commutativity

H^{0}(F)\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}=\sigma_{\mathscr{B}}\circ H^{0}(F)|
,
and
\mathbf{j}_{\mathscr{B}}=Q_{\mathscr{B}}\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{B}}
.
In the following, we simply set
\mathbf{L}(F):=\mathbf{L}(H^{0}(F))
(e.g., see Theorem
9.1
).

Let

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{F}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})
be dg functors with

{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}
small dg categories.
Then we can definie a natural transformation

\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F}\colon\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F)\to\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F)
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F}=(Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime})\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ(H^{0}F\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}):

(7.17)

\displaystyle\overbrace{Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}}}^{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})}\circ\overbrace{Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ H^{0}F\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}}^{\mathbf{L}(F)}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\overbrace{Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ(H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})}\circ H^{0}F)\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}}^{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F)},

where
\varepsilon_{{\mathscr{B}}}\colon(\sigma_{\mathscr{B}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}})\circ Q_{\mathscr{B}}\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})}
is the the counit.
Then in general, the equality
(
7.16
)
does not need to hold.
There are following two special cases.

Case 1. In the case that
F
preserves homotopically projective objects.
In this case, the equality
(
7.16
)
holds,
Indeed,

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F)

\displaystyle=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ H^{0}F\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ(H^{0}F|)\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ\mathbf{j}_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ(H^{0}F|)\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{B}}}\circ(H^{0}F|)\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ H^{0}F^{\prime}\circ H^{0}F\circ\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}=\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F).

Case 2. In the case that
F^{\prime}
preserves acyclic objects (hence preserves quasi-isomorphisms).
In this case,

\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F}\colon\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F)\ \raise 3.44444pt\hbox{${}^{\sim}$}\kern-6.99997pt\hbox{$\to$}\ \mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F)
is an isomorphism
because for each object
M\in{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
,
we have

(\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F})_{M}=Q_{{\mathscr{C}}}(H^{0}F^{\prime}(\varepsilon_{{\mathscr{B}},H^{0}F(\sigma_{{\mathscr{A}}}(\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{A}}}(M)))}),

which turns out to be an isomorphism.

To make the remark above more precisely, we introduce the following three 2-categories

\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
and
\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

that have more 1-morphisms than
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
{\mathscr{C}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
and
{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
have, resplectively.

Definition 7.23
.

We define
2
-categories
\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

and
\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
as follows:

•

\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}:=\{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}\}
.

•

For any objects
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}}),{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})
of
\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,

1
-morphisms from
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
to
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})
are the dg functors

F\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})
with
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}

(here we do not assume that they satisfy (
7.15
)).

•

\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}:=\{{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}\}
.

•

For any objects
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}}),{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
of
\tilde{{\mathscr{C}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,

1
-morphisms from
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})
to
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
are the
\Bbbk
-functors

F\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
.

•

\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})_{0}:=\{{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}\}
.

•

For any objects
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}}),{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
of
\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
),

1
-morphisms from
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})
to
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
are
the
\Bbbk
-functors of the form
\underline{F}\,(:=\mathrm{st}(F))

for some
\Bbbk
-functors
F\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{C}}({\mathscr{B}})
.

•

In each
2
-category, the
2
-morphisms are the natural transformations between those
1
-morphisms.

The following makes up Remark
7.22
.

Proposition 7.24
.

The
2
-functor
H^{0}\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
is extended to
a
2
-functor
H^{0}\colon\tilde{\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and
the pseudofunctor
\mathbf{L}\colon{\mathscr{H}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
is extended to
a lax functor
\mathbf{L}\colon\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
.

Proof.

The first assertion is straightforward.
For the second assertion, we summarize the structures of the lax functor
\mathbf{L}
.
For dg functors

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{F}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})
with

{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}
small dg categories.
The structure morphism
\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F}\colon\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F)\to\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F)
is given in
(
7.17
).
Also, for each
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{A}})\in\tilde{{\mathscr{H}}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, we have a natural isomorphism

\eta_{\mathscr{A}}\colon 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}\Longrightarrow Q_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}=Q_{\mathscr{A}}\circ H^{0}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(A)})\circ\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}=\mathbf{L}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})})

given by the unit of the adjoint
\sigma_{\mathscr{A}}\circ\mathbf{p}_{\mathscr{A}}\dashv Q_{\mathscr{A}}
, which defines the remaining structure of the lax functor
\mathbf{L}
.
It is easy to check that
\mathbf{L}
is in fact a lax functor which satisfies the axioms:

(i)

For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
the following are commutative:

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 23.5938pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.5938pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise (ii)

For dg functors

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})\xrightarrow{F}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})\xrightarrow{F^{\prime\prime}}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{E}})
with

{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}},{\mathscr{C}}
and
{\mathscr{E}}
small dg categories. The following is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime},F}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{L}F^{\prime\prime}\circ\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime}\circ F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{L}_{F^{\prime\prime},(F^{\prime},F)}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime\prime}\circ F^{\prime})\circ\mathbf{L}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{L}_{(F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}),F}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{L}(F^{\prime\prime}\circ F^{\prime}\circ F).}

∎

Definition 7.25
.

(1) The pseudofunctor
{\mathscr{H}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}

restricts to a pseudofunctor
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI}

sending
{\mathscr{A}}
to
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})
for all
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
.

(2)
For each
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
, we define

\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
to be the smallest full triangulated subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
closed under direct summands (and isomorphisms), and containing the representable functors
{\mathscr{A}}(\operatorname{-},M)
for all
M\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
.
Hence note that the class of objects of
\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
coincides with the class of compact objects in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
by a theorem explained in
[
28
, Sect. 5]
.

\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
is called the
perfect derived category
of
{\mathscr{A}}
.
Then the pseudofunctor
{\mathscr{D}}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
restricts to
a pseudofunctor
\operatorname{per}\colon\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
sending
{\mathscr{A}}
to
\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
for all
{\mathscr{A}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
.

We cite the following theorem from
[
8
]
,
which is a useful tool to define new colax functors from an old one by composing with pseudofunctors.

Theorem 7.26
.

Let
\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
be
2
-categories and
V\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a
pseudofunctor.
Then the obvious correspondence

\operatorname{Colax}(\mathbf{B},V)\colon\operatorname{Colax}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})\to\operatorname{Colax}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D})

turns out to be a pseudofunctor.

Corollary 7.27
.

Let
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}

be a colax functor.
Then the following are colax functors again

\displaystyle\text{The dg colax functor of }X:\ {\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X):={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{DGCAT},

\displaystyle\text{The complex colax functor of }X:\ \operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X):=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB},

\displaystyle\text{The homotopy colax functor of }X:\ {\mathscr{H}}(X):={\mathscr{H}}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI},

\displaystyle\text{The homotopically projecitve colax functor of }X:\ \operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X):=\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{TRI},

\displaystyle\text{The derived colax functor of }X:\ {\mathscr{D}}(X):={\mathscr{D}}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\text{The perfect derived colax functor of }X:\ \operatorname{per}(X):=\operatorname{per}\circ X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}.

Remark 7.28
.

Let
X=(X,X_{i},X_{b,a})\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.

(1) An explicit description of the complex colax functor

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X):=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}\circ X=(\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X),\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)_{i},\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)_{b,a})\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{FRB}

of
X
is given as follows.

•

for each
i\in I_{0}
,

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)(i)=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X(i))
; and

•

for each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, the functor

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)(a)\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)(i)\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)(j)

is given by
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}(X)(a)=\text{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}
,
where
\overline{X(a)}
is the
X(i)
-
X(j)
-bimodule

\overline{X(a)}:={}_{X(a)}X(j)
(Notation
7.7
(3)).

(2) An explicit description of the derived colax functor

{\mathscr{D}}(X)\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
of
X
is as follows.

•

for each
i\in I_{0}
,

{\mathscr{D}}(X)(i)={\mathscr{D}}(X(i))
; and

•

For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
,

{\mathscr{D}}(X)(a)\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X)(i)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)(j)

is given by

\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}:=\mathbf{L}(\text{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))\to{\mathscr{D}}(X(j)).

Note that by the remark in Definition
7.25
(2),
\operatorname{per}(X)
is
a colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{D}}(X)
.

Remark 7.29
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
.
Then it is obvious by definitions that

\Delta(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{C}}))=\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta(\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{C}}))=\operatorname{per}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}})).

Proposition 7.30
.

The pseudofunctor
\operatorname{per}
preserves
I
-precoverings, that is,
if
(F,\psi)\colon X\to\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
is an
I
-precovering in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

with
{\mathscr{C}}\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
, then
so is

\operatorname{per}(F,\psi)\colon\operatorname{per}(X)\to\Delta(\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{C}}))

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}).

Proof.

Let
i,j\in I_{0}
and
M\in(\operatorname{per}X(i))_{0},N\in(\operatorname{per}X(j))_{0}
.
It suffices to show that
\operatorname{per}(F,\psi)
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{per}(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{M,N}\colon\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{per}X(j)(M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},N)\to\operatorname{per}{\mathscr{C}}(M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)}).

By assumption,
(F,\psi)
induces an isomorphism

(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{x,y}\colon\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)

\to{\mathscr{C}}(F(i)x,F(j)y)

for all
x\in X(i)_{0},y\in X(j)_{0}
.
Namely,

(F,\psi)^{(1)}\colon\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)_{X(a)}\to{}_{F(j)}{\mathscr{C}}_{F(i)}

is a morphism of
X(j)
-
X(i)
-bimodules.
We first show the following.

Claim
.

There exists an isomorphism

\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}}(\overline{F(i)},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)})\to\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{X(j)}(\overline{X(a)},N)).

Indeed,
this is given by the composite of the following isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}}(\overline{F(i)},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}}({}_{F(i)}{\mathscr{C}},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}{}_{F(j)}{\mathscr{C}})

\displaystyle\overset{(\rm a)}{\to}N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}{}_{F(j)}{\mathscr{C}}_{F(i)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\rm b)}{\to}N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)_{X(a)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\rm c)}{\to}\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}X(j)_{X(a)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\rm d)}{\to}\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}N_{X(a)}

\displaystyle\overset{(\rm e)}{\to}\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{X(j)}({}_{X(a)}X(j),N)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{X(j)}(\overline{X(a)},N),

where (a) is obtained by the Yoneda lemma, (b) is an isomorphism,
induced from
((F,\psi)^{(1)})^{-1}
, (c) is the natural isomorphism induced by the cocontinuity of the tensor product,
(d) comes from the property of the tensor product, and
(e) is given by the Yoneda lemma.
Now, it is not hard to verify the commutativity of the following diagram:

\textstyle{\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{per}X(j)(M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},N)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{per}{\mathscr{C}}(M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)})}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{per}X(i)(M,\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{X(j)}(\overline{X(a)},N))}
\textstyle{\operatorname{per}X(i)(M,\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{C}}}(\overline{F(i)},N\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)})}
\textstyle{\operatorname{per}X(i)(M,\displaystyle\coprod_{a\in I(i,j)}\mathbf{R}\operatorname{Hom}_{X(j)}(\overline{X(a)},N)),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{per}(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{M,N}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\rm a)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\rm b)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\rm c)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\rm d)}

where the isomorphisms (a) and (b) are given by adjoints,
and (c) is the natural morphism, which is an isomorphism because
M
is compact,
and (d) is an isomorphism given by the claim above.
Hence
\operatorname{per}(F,\psi)^{(1)}_{M,N}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 7.31
.

The homotopically projecitve colax functor
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}
induce a colax funtor

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P,\psi)\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X)\to\Delta(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\textstyle\int X))

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}).

Proof.

For each
i\in I_{0}
, if
U
is in
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X(i))
,
then

\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P(i))(U)\cong U\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{P(i)}\\
=U\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

Therefore, if
Y
is an acyclic
\textstyle\int X
-module,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P(i))(U),Y)\cong{\mathscr{H}}(\textstyle\int X)(U\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?)),Y)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathscr{H}}(X(i))(U,{\mathscr{H}}(\textstyle\int X)((\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?)),Y))=0.

Then
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P(i))(U)
is in
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\textstyle\int X)
.
For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I_{1}
, the dg natural transformation

\phi(a)\colon P(i)\Rightarrow P(j)X(a)

\textstyle{X(i)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int X}
\textstyle{X(j)}
\textstyle{\textstyle\int X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P(i)}
\scriptstyle{P(j)}
\scriptstyle{X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\phi(a)}

is defined by
\phi(a)x:=(\delta_{b,a}1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{X(a)x})_{b\in I(i,j)}
for all
x\in X(i)_{0}
, then we have the following diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X(i))}
\textstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\textstyle\int X)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X(j))}
\textstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\textstyle\int X).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P(i))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(P(j))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X(a))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(\phi(a))}

∎

Definition 7.32

(Quasi-equivalences
[
30
]
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}
be small dg categories and

E\colon{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{B}}
a dg functor.
Then
E
is called a
quasi-equivalence
if

(1)

The restriction

E_{X,Y}\colon{\mathscr{A}}(X,Y)\to{\mathscr{B}}(E(X),E(Y))
of
E
to
{\mathscr{A}}(X,Y)
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
X,Y\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
; and

(2)

The induced functor
H^{0}(E):H^{0}({\mathscr{A}})\to H^{0}({\mathscr{B}})
is an equivalence.

Remark 7.33
.

By definition, it is clear that the relation defined by quasi-equivalence is reflexive and transitive, but
it is known to be not symmetric.

Definition 7.34
.

For a triangulated category
{\mathscr{U}}
and a class of objects
{\mathscr{V}}
in
{\mathscr{U}}
,
we denote by
\operatorname{thick}_{\mathscr{U}}{\mathscr{V}}
(resp.
\operatorname{Loc}_{\mathscr{U}}{\mathscr{V}}
)
the smallest full triangulated subcategory of
{\mathscr{U}}

closed under direct summands (resp. infinite direct sums) that contains
{\mathscr{V}}
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
be a small dg category, and
{\mathscr{T}}
a full dg subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
Then
{\mathscr{T}}
is called a
tilting dg subcategory
for
{\mathscr{A}}
, if

(1)

{\mathscr{T}}_{0}\subseteq\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}\bigcap\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
,
(hence every
T\in\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})_{0}
is a compact object in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
4
4

4

Note that an object
T
of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
is in
\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
if and only if
T
is a compact object in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
by Neeman’s theorem
[
28
, Theorem 5.3]
.
); and

(2)

\operatorname{thick}_{{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}({\mathscr{T}}_{0})=\operatorname{per}({\mathscr{A}})
(equivalently
\operatorname{Loc}_{{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}({\mathscr{T}})={\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
).

Thus, in Keller’s words in
[
28
]
,
{\mathscr{T}}
is tilting
if and only if
{\mathscr{T}}_{0}
forms a set of small (= compact) generators for
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

8.
Derived equivalences of colax functors

In this section, we define necessary terminologies such as
2
-quasi-isomorphisms for
2
-morphisms,
quasi-equivalences for
1
-morphisms, and the derived
1
-morphism

\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})\colon

{\mathscr{D}}(X)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
of a
1
-morphism

(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
between colax functors,
and show the fact that the derived
1
-morphism of a quasi-equivalence between colax functors
X
,
X^{\prime}

turns out to be an equivalence between derived dg module colax functors of
X
,
X^{\prime}
.
Finally, we give definitions of tilting subfunctors and of derived equivalences.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a
2
-category and
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}

a
1
-morphism in the
2
-category
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbf{C})
.
Then
(F,\psi)
is called
I
-
equivariant

if for each
a\in I_{1}
,
\psi(a)
is a
2
-isomorphism in
\mathbf{C}
.

We cite the following without a proof.

Lemma 8.2

(
[
7
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a
2
-category and
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}

a
1
-morphism in the
2
-category
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbf{C})
.
Then
(F,\psi)
is an equivalence in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\mathbf{C})

if and only if

(1)

For each
i\in I_{0}
,
F(i)

is an equivalence in
\mathbf{C}
; and

(2)

For each
a\in I_{1}
,
\psi(a)
is a
2
-isomorphism in
\mathbf{C}

(
namely,
(F,\psi)
is
I
-equivariant
)
.

To define the notion of
2
-quasi-isomorphisms in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}

, we need the following statement.

We adapt Notation
7.7
(3), e.g.,
\overline{E}:={}_{E}{\mathscr{B}}
,
\overline{\alpha}:={}_{\alpha}{\mathscr{B}}

and
\overline{E}^{*}:={\mathscr{B}}_{E},\overline{\alpha}^{*}:={\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha}
.
Note that since
\alpha
is a dg natural transformation,
both
\overline{\alpha}
and
\overline{\alpha}^{*}
are
0
-cocyle morphisms
by Remark
7.6
, and
hence it is possible to define
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{\alpha}
,
H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}
and so on.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{\alpha}
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{\alpha}}

is a
2
-isomorphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
.

(2)

H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}\colon{}_{E(x)}{\mathscr{B}}\to{}_{F(x)}{\mathscr{B}}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})

for all
x\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
.

(3)

\overline{\alpha}^{*}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\text{-}
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}}^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}^{*}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\text{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{E}^{*}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\text{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}^{*}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\text{-}}

is a
2
-isomorphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
.

(4)

H^{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{x}}\colon{\mathscr{B}}_{E(x)}\to{\mathscr{B}}_{F(x)}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{H}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).
Let
x\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
.
Note that we have

{}_{x}{\mathscr{A}}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{\alpha}\cong Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}
, which is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
if and only if

H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
Hence (2) follows from (1) by applying (1) to the representable functor
{}_{x}{\mathscr{A}}
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).
Let
{\mathscr{U}}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
consisting of objects
M
satisfying the condition
that
M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{\alpha}\colon M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{E}\to M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{F}
is an isomorphism.
Then by (2) we have
{}_{x}{\mathscr{A}}\in{\mathscr{U}}
for all
x\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
.
Here, it is easy to show that

{\mathscr{U}}
is a triangulated subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
and that

{\mathscr{U}}
is closed under isomorphisms and direct sums with
small index sets.
Therefore we have
{\mathscr{U}}={\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
,
which means that (1) holds.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(4).
Assume that

H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}\colon{}_{E(x)}{\mathscr{B}}\to{}_{F(x)}{\mathscr{B}}

is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{H}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
Then
Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
We set
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(\cdot,\operatorname{-}):={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})(\cdot,\operatorname{-})
.
Then the functor

\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(\cdot,{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}):=Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}H^{0}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})(\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(\cdot),{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}})\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})

sends the isomorphism
Q_{{\mathscr{B}}}H^{0}{}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}}
to an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}})

\displaystyle\colon\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{F(x)}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}})

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{E(x)}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}),

in
{\mathscr{D}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})
,
which is given by

\displaystyle Q_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}}H^{0}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{\alpha_{x}}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}})

\displaystyle\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{F(x)}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{E(x)}{\mathscr{B}},{}_{{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathscr{B}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}),

in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}})
(see Remark
7.21
for
H^{0}
).
By the Yoneda lemma, it is isomorphic to

Q_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}}H^{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{x}}\colon{\mathscr{B}}_{F(x)}\to{\mathscr{B}}_{E(x)}

and is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})
.
As a consequence,
H^{0}{\mathscr{B}}_{\alpha_{x}}
is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{H}}({{\mathscr{B}}}^{\mathrm{op}})
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(2).
This is proved in the same way as in the converse direction.

(3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4).
The same proof for the equivalence (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) works
also for this case.
∎

Definition 8.4
.

Let
E,F\colon{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{B}}
be
1
-morphisms and

\alpha\colon E\Rightarrow F

a
2
-morphism in the
2
-category
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
.
Then
\alpha
is called a
2
-quasi-isomorphism
in

\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
if one of the statements (1), …, (4) in Lemma
8.3
holds.

Remark 8.5
.

We can use the condition (2) above to check whether

\alpha
is a
2
-quasi-equivalence.
Once it is checked, we can use the property (1).

Definition 8.6
.

Let
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a
1
-morphism in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then
(F,\psi)
is called a
quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism
if

(1)

For each
i\in I_{0}
,
F(i):X(i)\to X^{\prime}(i)

is a quasi-equivalence; and

(2)

For each
a\in I_{1}
,
\psi(a)
is a 2-quasi-isomorphism (see Definition
8.4
).

See the diagram below to understand the situation:

\textstyle{X(i)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(i)}
\textstyle{X(j)}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(j).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X(a)}
\scriptstyle{X^{\prime}(a)}
\scriptstyle{F(i)}
q-eq
\scriptstyle{F(j)}
q-eq
2-qis
\scriptstyle{\psi(a)}

Remark 8.7
.

In the above, consider the condition

(2^{\prime})

For each
a\in I_{1}
,
\psi(a)
is a
2
-isomorphiusm.

Then obviously
(2^{\prime})
implies
(2)
.
Therefore, a
1
-morphism
(F,\psi)
satisfying (1) and
(2^{\prime})
can be called
an
I
-equivariant quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism
.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and

(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X)\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime})
be in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
.
Then we define a
1
-morphism

\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi}):=(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F},\mathbf{L}\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
as follows.
For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
,
(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F})(i):=\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F}(i))
, and
(\mathbf{L}\boldsymbol{\psi})(a)
is
defined by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}((\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\circ\mathbf{F}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbf{L}\boldsymbol{\psi})(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}(\boldsymbol{\psi}(a))}

where the vertical arrow on the right is the identity
(see Case 1 in Remark
7.22
), and that
on the left is

\mathbf{L}_{(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}),\mathbf{F}(i)}
, the structure morphism of the lax functor
\mathbf{L}
(see Remark
7.22
),
which turns out to be the identity if
\mathbf{F}(i)
preserves homotopically projective objects.

Definition 8.9
.

Let
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be
a 1-morhism
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
This yields a
1
-morphism

(\dot{F},\dot{\psi}):=((\dot{F}(i))_{i\in I_{0}},(\dot{\psi}(a))_{a\in I_{1}})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime}),

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
, which defines a 1-morphism

\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})={\mathscr{D}}((F,\psi))\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.
The explicit forms of
(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})
and
\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi}):=(\mathbf{L}\dot{F},\mathbf{L}\dot{\psi})
are given as follows.
For each
i\in I_{0}
, using the dg
X(i)
-
X^{\prime}(i)
-bimodule
\overline{F(i)}:={}_{F(i)}X^{\prime}(i)
, we define
a dg functor

\dot{F}(i):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{F(i)}\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime}(i)).

Note here that the bimodule
\overline{F(i)}
is right homotopically projective.
Hence by Lemma
7.18
,
\dot{F}(i)
preserves homotopically projective objects.
This defines a triangle functor

(\mathbf{L}\dot{F})(i):=\mathbf{L}(\dot{F}(i))=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)}\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i)).

Next let
a\colon i\to j
be a morphism in
I
.
Then
\psi(a)\colon X^{\prime}(a)F(i)\Rightarrow F(j)X(a)

induces a morphism of
X^{\prime}(j)
-
X(i)
-bimodules

\overline{\psi(a)}\colon\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}\to\overline{F(j)X(a)},

where we adapt Notation
7.7
(3),
e.g.,
\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}:={}_{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}X^{\prime}(j)
,
which induces the diagram

\textstyle{(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{{X(a)}})\otimes_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{F(j)X(a)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{\psi}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

(8.18)

of 2-morphisms in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, where the vertical morphisms are natural isomorphisms.
Then
\dot{\psi}(a)
is defined as the
unique 2-morphism making this diagram commutative, which is usually identified with

\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}
.
This gives us the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(j))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime}(j)).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{F}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\dot{F}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(a))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\dot{\psi}(a)}

and the
1
-morphism
(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
7.20
,
the pseudofunctor
\mathbf{L}\circ H^{0}
sends the diagram (
8.18
) to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{{X(a)}})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(j)}\overline{F(j)}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(j)X(a)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{L}(\dot{\psi}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

(8.19)

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
.
Using this we set

(\mathbf{L}\dot{\psi})(a):=\mathbf{L}(\dot{\psi}(a))\colon(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\mathbf{L}\dot{F}(i)\Rightarrow\mathbf{L}\dot{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}),

which gives us the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X(j))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j)).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbf{L}\dot{F})(i)}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbf{L}\dot{F})(j)}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}={\mathscr{D}}(X(a))}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}={\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbf{L}\dot{\psi})(a)}

and the
1
-morphism
(\mathbf{L}\dot{F},\mathbf{L}\dot{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
.

The following says that a quasi-equivalence between colax functors
induces a derived equivlence between them, which will be important for our main result.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a quasi-equivalence
1-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,

\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then

\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})

is an equivalence in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
i\in I_{0}
.
Then since
F(i)\colon X(i)\to X^{\prime}(i)
is a quasi-equivalence,
we have

(\mathbf{L}\dot{F})(i):=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(i)}\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))

is an equivalence of triangulated categories by Theorem
A.1
.

Let
a\colon i\to j
be a morphism in
I
.
Then since

\psi(a)\colon X^{\prime}(a)F(i)\Rightarrow F(j)X(a)

is a 2-quasi-isomorphism,
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}
is a
2
-isomorphism by definition.
Hence by the commutative diagram (
8.19
),

(\mathbf{L}\dot{\psi})(a)\colon(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\mathbf{L}\dot{F}(i)\Rightarrow\mathbf{L}\dot{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}).

is a 2-isomorphism.
Therefore, by Lemma
8.2
,
\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})
is an equivalence in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.
∎

A dg
\Bbbk
-category
{\mathscr{A}}
is called
\Bbbk
-
projective

(resp.
\Bbbk
-
flat
) if

{\mathscr{A}}(x,y)
are dg projective (resp. flat)
\Bbbk
-modules for all
x,y\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}
.

Definition 8.11
.

Let
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
be a colax functor.

(1)

X
is called
\Bbbk
-
projective
(resp.
\Bbbk
-
flat
)
if
X(i)
are
\Bbbk
-projective (resp.
\Bbbk
-flat) for all
i\in I_{0}
.

(2)

Let
Y,Y^{\prime}\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT}
be colax functors.
Then
Y^{\prime}
is called a
colax subfunctor
of
Y
if
there exists a
I
-
equivariant inclusion

1
-morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y
,
namely, a
1
-morphism
(\sigma,\rho)\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y

such that
\sigma(i)\colon Y^{\prime}(i)\to Y(i)
is the inclusion for each
i\in I_{0}
, and
\rho(a)\colon Y(a)\sigma(i)\Rightarrow\sigma(j)Y^{\prime}(a)
is
an
2
-isomorphism (i.e., a dg natural isomorphism)
for each morphism
a\colon i\to j
in
I
.

(3)

A colax subfunctor
{\mathscr{T}}
of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

is called a
tilting colax functor
for
X

if for each
i\in I_{0}
,

{\mathscr{T}}(i)\subseteq{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))
is a tilting dg subcategory for
X(i)
(see Definition
7.34
).
See the diagram below for
(\sigma,\rho)
:

\textstyle{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(j)).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(i)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(j)}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(a))}
\scriptstyle{\rho(a)}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

Definition 8.12
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then
X
and
X^{\prime}
are said to be
derived equivalent

(we denoted this fact by
X\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}X
)
if

{\mathscr{D}}(X)
and
{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
are equivalent
in the 2-category
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.
Note by Lemma
8.2
that this is the case if and only if
there exists a
1
-morphism

(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi}):{\mathscr{D}}(X)\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})

such that

(1)

For each
i\in I_{0}
,
\mathbf{F}(i):{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))\to{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))
is a triangle equivalence in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}
; and

(2)

For each
a\in I_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(a)
is a 2-isomorphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2}

(
i.e.,
(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})
is
I
-equivariant
)
.

In the next section, we will characterize
a derived equivalence between colax functors in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

given by a left derived functor between dg module categories
or given by the left derived tensor functor of a bimodule.

9.
Characterizations of standard derived equivalences of colax functors

In this section,
we define standard derived equivalences between colax functors
from
I
to
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
, and give its characterizations as
our first main result in this paper.

We first cite the following from
[
28
, Theorem 8.1]
without a proof.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}
be small dg categories.
Consider the following conditions.

(1)

There is a dg functor
H:{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
such that
\mathbf{L}H:{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
is an equivalence
(
see Remark
\ref{rmk:L-dgfun})
.

(2)

{\mathscr{C}}
is quasi-equivalent to a tilting dg subcategory for
{\mathscr{A}}
.

(3)

There exists a dg category
{\mathscr{B}}
and dg functors

{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})\xrightarrow{G}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{B}})\xrightarrow{F}{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})

such that
\mathbf{L}G
and
\mathbf{L}F
are equivalences.

Then

(a)

(1)
implies
(2)
.

(b)

(2)
implies
(3)
.

Next, we cite the statement
[
28
, Theorem 8.2]
in the
\Bbbk
-flat case.

Theorem 9.2

(Keller)
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be small dg
\Bbbk
-categories and assume that

{\mathscr{A}}
is
\Bbbk
-flat.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

There exists a
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{A}}
-bimodue
Y
such that

\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathscr{B}}Y:{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})

is a triangle equivalence.

(2)

There is a dg functor
H:{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
such that
\mathbf{L}H:{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
is an equivalence
(
see Remark
\ref{rmk:L-dgfun})
.

(3)

{\mathscr{B}}
is quasi-equivalent to a tilting dg subcategory for
{\mathscr{A}}
.

The following lemma given by Keller characterizes dg bimdoules which induced an equivalences of derived categories.

Lemma 9.3

(
[
30
, Lemma 3.10]
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories and
E
an
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
bimodule.
Then

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}E\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})

is an equivalence of triangulated categories,
if and only if

(1)

the dg
{\mathscr{B}}
-module
E(\operatorname{-},A)
is perfect for all
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
,

(2)

the morphism

{\mathscr{A}}(A,A^{\prime})\to\mathbf{R}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}})(E(\operatorname{-},A),E(\operatorname{-},A^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism for all
A,A^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{A}}
and

(3)

the dg
{\mathscr{B}}
-module
E(\operatorname{-},A)
,
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
, form a tilting dg subcategory for
{\mathscr{B}}

(Definition
7.34
).

Definition 9.4
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories and
E
an
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
bimodule.
We denote by
\underline{{\mathscr{A}}}
the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})

with

\underline{{\mathscr{A}}}_{0}=\{D\in{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\mid D\cong C^{\wedge}\text{ for some }C\in{\mathscr{A}}_{0}\}.

Then
E
is called a
quasi-equivalence bimodule

if (a)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}E\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})

is an equivalence of triangulated categories,
and (b) it gives rise to an equivalence

\underline{{\mathscr{A}}}\to\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}
, that is,

\underline{{\mathscr{A}}}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}E\subseteq\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}
.

Definition 9.5
.

The derived equivalence of the form

\mathbf{L}H\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
or

\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathscr{B}}Y\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
above
is called a
standard derived equivalence
from
{\mathscr{B}}
to
{\mathscr{A}}
, and if the statements of Theorem
9.2
hold,
then we say that
{\mathscr{B}}
is
standardly derived equivalent
to
{\mathscr{A}}
. Here it seems that
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
are not symmetric,
but in that case, the
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule
Y^{T}:={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})(Y,{\mathscr{A}})
induces a triangle equivalence

\text{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}Y^{T}:{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
Thus this relation is symmetric for
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
.

Definition 9.6
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.

(1)

An
X^{\prime}
-
X
-
bimodule
is a pair
Z=((Z(i))_{i\in I_{0}},(Z(a))_{a\in I_{1}})
,
where

Z(i)
is an
X^{\prime}(i)
-
X(i)
-bimodule for all
i\in I_{0}
, and

Z(a)\colon Z(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\to\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}Z(j)

is a
0
-cocycle morphism of
X^{\prime}(i)
-
X(j)
-bimodules
for all morphisms
a\colon i\to j
in
I
,
such that

\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}}Z:=((\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i))_{i\in I_{0}},(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(\operatorname{dom}(a))}Z(a))_{a\in I_{1}})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

is a
1
-morphism in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, where
\operatorname{dom}(a)=i
is the domain of
a\in I_{1}
, and

\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a)
is given up to associators (see Definition
7.8
),
i.e., it is identified with the composite
with associators as in the diagram

(2)

If
{}_{B}Z(i)
is a homotopically projective dg
X(i)
-module for all
B\in X^{\prime}(i)_{0}

and
i\in I_{0}
, then
Z
is said to be
right homotopically projective
.

(3)

A
1
-morphism
(F,\psi)\colon X^{\prime}\to X
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})

is said to
preserve homotopically projective objects
if so does
F(i)
for all
i\in I_{0}
.

Recall that there is another definition of quasi-equivalences between dg categories by using “quasi-functor” given by Keller.
We will give a similar definition in our setting.

Definition 9.7
.

X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and

be a

-

-bimodule.

Namely, it is required that for any
x\in X^{\prime}(i)_{0}
,
there exists some
y\in X(i)_{0}
such that

Z(\operatorname{-},x)\cong x^{\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z\cong y^{\wedge}
.

(4)

The
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
is called a
quasi-equivalence bimodule
if (a)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)

is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
, and (b) it gives rise to an equivalence
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{X}
.
Here in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}X^{\prime}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma(j)}

the condition (b) is equivalent to saying that

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i)\colon\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)\to\underline{X}(i)

is an equivalence for all
i\in I_{0}
, and
that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a)
in the bottom square
is a 2-isomorphism for all
a\colon i\to j
in
I_{1}
.
Note that it also required that

(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a))\circ\sigma^{\prime}(i)=\sigma(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a))
for all morphisms
a\colon i\to j
in
I

(note that both hand sides are horizontal composites),
but this is automatically satisfied

5
5

5

The left/right faces, and the front/back faces are strictly commutative by (3) and (1), respectively
.

Remark 9.8
.

Note that in Definition
8.6

another notion of
quasi-equivalence
is defined
for a
1
-morphsim, which we have to distinguish.
Any quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism
X\to X^{\prime}
induces
a quasi-equivalence
X
-
X^{\prime}
-bimdoule by Proposition
8.10
.

Indeed, in this proposition,
the
X
-
X^{\prime}
-bimodule

E:=(\overline{F(i)},\overline{\psi(a)})_{i\in I_{0},a\in I_{1}}
is equal to

\mathbf{L}(\dot{F},\dot{\psi})
and
is a quasi-equivalence bimodule.

Lemma 9.9
.

The following are equivalent

(1)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
giving rise to an equivalence
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{X}
.

(2)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z
gives rise to equivalences
{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X}
and
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{X}
.

In this case,
there exists a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}\to X
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2). This is trivial by the definition of quasi-equivalence.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).
Assume that the statement (2) holds.
To show that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
, it remains that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a))
is a
2
-natural isomorphism. It follows that
C^{\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(a)
is invertible for all
C\in X^{\prime}(i)
by the assumption.

∎

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, and

Z
a
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule.
Then for any
a\colon i\to j
in
I
,

Z
naturally induces a
2
-morphism

\widehat{Z(a)}\colon(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\Rightarrow\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}).

Proof.

We first define a 2-morphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(j))(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},\operatorname{-})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-}).

This is defined as a composite of the following 2-morphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\operatorname{-}))

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}Z(j),\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(j))(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(j))(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},\operatorname{-})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-}),

where
(*)
stands for
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(M(a),\operatorname{-})
, and
the isomorphisms are given by adjunctions.

Then we can define
\widehat{Z(a)}
as the composite of the following
(the second and the third ones are obtained by
applying the functors
(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})
from the left, and

(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})
from the right to the above.)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\circ 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(j))(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},\operatorname{-})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(c)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}),

where (a) is given by the unit, (b) is given by the 2-morphism
defined above, and (c) is given by the counit.
∎

Definition 9.11
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
and let
{\mathscr{U}}
be a colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{D}}(X)

such that
{\mathscr{U}}(i)
is a full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))
for all
i\in I_{0}
.

(1)

We denote by
{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}
the colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{D}}(X)

such that
{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}(i)
is the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))

with
{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}(i)_{0}=\{U[n]\mid U\in{\mathscr{U}}(i)_{0},n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

(2)

A
lift
of
{\mathscr{U}}
is a a pair
(X^{\prime},Z)
of a colax functor
X^{\prime}
and an
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
such that

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
gives rise to equivalences

{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}
and
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathscr{U}}
.

(3)

A
standard lift
of
{\mathscr{U}}
is a lift
({\mathscr{B}},M)
of
{\mathscr{U}}
constructed as follows:
Take
{\mathscr{B}}
as the colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
such that
for each
i\in I
,
{\mathscr{B}}(i)
is the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))

with
{\mathscr{B}}(i)_{0}:=\{\mathbf{p}_{X(i)}U\mid U\in{\mathscr{U}}(i)_{0}\},{\mathscr{B}}(a):={\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(a))|_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}
for all
a\in I_{1}

and
M
as a
{\mathscr{B}}
-
X
-bimodule definied by

{}_{B}M(i)_{A}:=B(A)\cong{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))(A^{\wedge},B)
for all
B\in{\mathscr{B}}_{0},A\in X(i)_{0}
, and we define
a
0
-cocycle morphism

M(a)\colon M(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\to\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}M(j)

of
{\mathscr{B}}(i)
-
X(j)
-bimodules by

\displaystyle M(a)_{C^{\wedge}}\colon C^{\wedge}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}M(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\cong M(i)(\operatorname{-},C)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}

\displaystyle\cong C\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\cong{\mathscr{T}}(a)(C)\cong C^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes M(j)

for all
a\in I_{1}
and
C\in{\mathscr{B}}(i)
.

Remark 9.12
.

The definition of a lift implies that in particular that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z
induces an equivalence from
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime})
onto the colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
generated by
{\mathscr{U}}
, where
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime})
is a colax subfuncor of
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X^{\prime})
such that
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime}(i))
is the smallest strictly full subcategory of
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}{X^{\prime}(i)}
containing of the
A^{\wedge},A\in X^{\prime}(i)
for
i\in I
. If
Z(i)_{B}
is homotopically projective for each
B\in X^{\prime}(i)
, a quasi-inverse is induced by
\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),-)
.
We now define a 2-isomorphism

\widetilde{Z(a)}\colon(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{U}}(i)}{\mathscr{U}}(a))

as the converse of the composite of the following 2-isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j)(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ{\mathscr{U}}(a)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\Rightarrow}\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j)(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ{\mathscr{U}}(a)\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i))\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\Rightarrow}\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j)(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}Z(j))\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(a)}\overline{X(a)})\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(c)}}{\Rightarrow}(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(a)}\overline{X(a)})\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-}),

where (a) and (c) are induced by
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}\Rightarrow(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i))\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})
and

(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(a)}\overline{X(a)}\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-})\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}
,
respectively, and
(b) is given by

\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j)(Z(j),\operatorname{-})\circ Z(a)\circ\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(Z(i),\operatorname{-}).

Then we have the following diagram

We use the following notion to prove
Proposition
9.14
below.

Definition 9.13
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a small dg category, and
M

a (right) dg
{\mathscr{C}}
-module.
Then the
category
\mathbf{c}(M)
of elements
of
M
is a category
defined as follows.

For each
i\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
, recall that
M(i)
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
vector space
M(i)=\bigoplus_{p\in{\mathbb{Z}}}M(i)^{p}
with a differential

d_{M(i)}
of degree 1.
By forgetting both the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vector space structure and the differential, we regard
M(i)
just as a set, and we set

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}

\displaystyle:=\bigsqcup_{j\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}M(j)=\{j_{x}:=(j,x)\mid j\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0},x\in M(j)\},\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}(M)_{1}

\displaystyle:=\bigsqcup_{a\in{\mathscr{C}}_{1}}M(\operatorname{cod}(a))=\{a_{x}:=(a,x)\mid a\in{\mathscr{C}}_{1},x\in M(\operatorname{cod}(a))\}.

For each
a_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{1}
with
a\colon i\to j
in
{\mathscr{C}}
,
we set its source to be
s(a_{x}):=i_{M(a)(x)}
and
its target to be
t(a_{x}):=j_{x}
,
namely we regard
a_{x}
as an arrow

a_{x}\colon i_{M(a)(x)}\to j_{x}.

In this way we define a quiver
(\mathbf{c}(M)_{0},\mathbf{c}(M)_{1},s,t)
.
Let
i_{z}\xrightarrow{a_{y}}j_{y}\xrightarrow{b_{x}}k_{x}
be a path of length 2 in this quiver. Then by definition,
i\xrightarrow{a}j\xrightarrow{b}k
is in
{\mathscr{C}}
and

z=M(a)(y),y=M(b)(x)
,
thus we have
z=M(a)M(b)(x)=M(ba)(x)
.
Therefore, we have an arrow
(ba)_{x}\colon i_{z}\to k_{x}

in this quiver.
The composition is then defined by setting

b_{x}\circ a_{y}:=(ba)_{x}.

The identity of
j_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}
is given by
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j_{x}}=(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j})_{x}
.

We define a functor
P_{M}\colon\mathbf{c}(M)\to{\mathscr{C}}

as the first projection, namely by
sending
a_{x}\colon i_{M(a)(x)}\to j_{x}
to

a\colon i\to j
.

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a small dg category.
Then every dg
{\mathscr{C}}
-module is given as a colimit
of representable dg
{\mathscr{C}}
-modules.

Proof.

Let
Y_{\centerdot}\colon{\mathscr{C}}\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{C}})
be the dg Yoneda embedding,
which is defined by sending
a\colon i\to j
in
{\mathscr{C}}
to

{\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},a)\colon{\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},i)\to{\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},j)
.
Let
M
be a dg
{\mathscr{C}}
-module.
We show that
M
is isomorphic to the colimit of
Y_{\centerdot}\circ P_{M}
:

M\cong\varinjlim(Y_{\centerdot}\circ P_{M}).

For simplicity, we set
F:=Y_{\centerdot}\circ P_{M}
.
We first construct a cocone
(M,(\alpha_{j_{x}})_{j_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}})
.
For each
j_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}
with
j\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0},x\in M(j)
,
we define
\alpha_{j_{x}}\colon F(j_{x})\to M
as follows.
By definition, we have

F(j_{x})=(Y_{\centerdot}\circ P_{M})(j_{x})=Y_{\centerdot}(j)={\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},j).

(9.20)

By the dg Yoneda lemma, there exists an isomorphism

\phi_{j,M}\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{C}})({\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},j),M)\to M(j)
.
Then we define
\alpha_{j_{x}}
by

\alpha_{j_{x}}:=\phi_{j,M}^{-1}(x)=M(\operatorname{-})(x),

which sends any
a\colon i\to j
in
{\mathscr{C}}

to
\alpha_{j_{x}}(a):=M(a)(x)
,
in particular,
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{j}
to
x
.
We show that
(M,(\alpha_{j_{x}})_{j_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}})
is a cocone of
F
.
Let
\alpha_{x}\colon i_{y}\to j_{x}
be a morphism in
\mathbf{c}(M)
with

a\colon i\to j
in
{\mathscr{C}}
and
x\in M(j),y=M(a)(x)
.
Then by noting (
9.20
) and that

F(a_{x})={\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},a)
,
it is enough to show the commutativity of the diagram

\hbox to102.2pt{\vbox to84.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.46127pt\lower-42.39589pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

By the dg Yoneda lemma, it is enough to check the commutativity
for the
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}
, which is shown by

\alpha_{i_{y}}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})=y=M(a)(x)=\alpha_{j_{x}}(a)=\alpha_{j_{x}}({\mathscr{C}}(\operatorname{-},a)(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}))
.

Finally, we show that this cocone is a colimit.
Let
(N,(\beta_{i_{x}}\colon F(i_{x})\to N)_{i_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}})

be any cocone of
F
.
It is enough to show the unique existence of a
\gamma\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{C}})(M,N)
that makes the diagram

\hbox to121.97pt{\vbox to50.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.98634pt\lower-24.73613pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
