commutative for all
i_{x}\in\mathbf{c}(M)_{0}
.
By the commutativity for
1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}
, we must have

\gamma_{i}(x)=\beta_{i_{x}}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})

for all
i\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0},x\in M(i)
.
Conversely we can define a morphism
\gamma\colon M\to N
by
setting
\gamma_{i}(x):=\beta_{i_{x}}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})

for all
i\in{\mathscr{C}}_{0},x\in M(i)
, which makes the diagram
above commutative.
Thus the unique existence of such a
\gamma
is verified.
∎

Lemma 9.15
.

If
(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
is a
1
-morphism in the
2
-category

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
, then we can define an
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
N

by setting
N(i)(D,\linebreak C)=\mathbf{F}(i)(C^{\wedge})(D)
for
C\in X^{\prime}(i)
and
B\in X(i)
.

Proof.

Let
N(i)
be the bimodule
N(i)(D,C)=F(i)(C^{\wedge})(D)
for
C\in X^{\prime}(i)
and
B\in X(i)
. For any
C\in X^{\prime}(i)
, we define a 2-morphism

N(a)_{C^{\wedge}}\colon C^{\wedge}\otimes N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\to C^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}N(j)

as the composite
of the following 2-morphisms:

\displaystyle C^{\wedge}\otimes N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}

\displaystyle\cong N(i)(\operatorname{-},C)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}

\displaystyle=F(i)(C^{\wedge})\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{\psi(a)_{C^{\wedge}}}}{\Longrightarrow}F(j)(C^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})

\displaystyle\cong F(j)((X^{\prime}(a)C)^{\wedge})

\displaystyle=N(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)C)

\displaystyle\cong X^{\prime}(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)C)\otimes N(j)

\displaystyle\cong C^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}N(j)

By Proposition
9.14
,
every dg
X^{\prime}(i)
-module
T
is a colimit of representable functors,
we chose an isomorphism
\xi_{T}\colon\varinjlim T_{\lambda}\to T
with
(T_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
a family of representable functors once for all,
we can define
N(a)_{T}
by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim T_{\lambda}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim T_{\lambda}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}N(j),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim N(a)_{T_{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(a)_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varinjlim\xi_{T})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varinjlim\xi_{T})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and we obtain a 2-morphism

N(a):=(N(a)_{T})_{T\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(i))}\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\Rightarrow\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}N(j).

∎

Now let
({\mathscr{B}},M)
be any lift of
{\mathscr{U}}
such that
{}_{B}M(i)
is homotopically projective for each
B\in{\mathscr{B}}(i)
. Let
X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
and
(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
be a
1
-morphism in the
2
-category

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
such that
\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon

{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
induce a
1
-morphism
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathscr{U}}
.
Then we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{U}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{U}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{U}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{U}}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}F(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}X^{\prime}(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}{M(i)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}X(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}F(j)}

(9.21)

Keeping the notations above,
let
Y
be an
X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule given by

Y(i)(B,C)=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))({}_{B}M(i),F(i)(C^{\wedge}))

for
i\in I,B\in{\mathscr{B}}(i),C\in X^{\prime}(i)
.

By Lemma
9.10
, we have

\overline{Y(a)}\colon\operatorname{-}(\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(i))(M(i),\operatorname{-})\Rightarrow\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}).

Then we have the following

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)})\circ\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(i))(M(i),\operatorname{-})\circ\mathbf{F}(i)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(a)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\operatorname{-})\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})\circ\mathbf{F}(i)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(b)}}{\Rightarrow}

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\operatorname{-})\circ\mathbf{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}),

where (a) is given by

\overline{Y(a)}\circ\mathbf{F}(i)
, (b) is given by

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\operatorname{-})\circ\boldsymbol{\psi}(a)
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}(a)\colon(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})\circ\mathbf{F}(i)\Rightarrow\mathbf{F}(j)\circ(\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})
.

Now for
a\in I_{1}
, and any
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(i),b\in{\mathscr{B}}(i)
, we have

\displaystyle(x^{\prime\wedge})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}\cong Y(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(M(i),F(i)(x^{\prime\wedge}))\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(c)}}{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\operatorname{-})\circ\mathbf{F}(j)\circ((x^{\prime\wedge})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))(M(j),\mathbf{F}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime})^{\wedge}))

\displaystyle\cong(x^{\prime\wedge})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j)

where (c) is given the above morphism. By Lemma
9.15
, we have a bimodule morphism

Y(a)\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}\\
\to\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j).

Lemma 9.16
.

(1)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Y
induces a
1
-morphism
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}
, hence
Y
is a quasi-functor. It is a quasi-equivalence if
\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}
induces an equivalence
{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}
.

(2)

There is a canonical
2
-morphism

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Y\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}}M\to\mathbf{L}F,

which is invertible for
Q\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime}(i))
, that is,

Q\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}M(i)\ \raise 3.44444pt\hbox{${}^{\sim}$}\kern-6.99997pt\hbox{$\to$}\ \mathbf{L}F(i)(Q)

where
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime}(i))
is the smallest strictly full subcategory of
\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X^{\prime}(i))
containing of the
A^{\wedge},A\in X^{\prime}(i)
.
It is invertible for arbitrary
Q\in{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))
if and only if
\mathbf{L}F(i)
commutes with direct sums for each
i\in I_{0}
.

(3)

If
(X^{\prime},Z)
is a lift of
{\mathscr{U}}
and
F=\operatorname{-}\otimes Z
, then
Y
is a quasi-equivalence bimodule
X^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{B}}
and we have
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Y\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}}M\cong\mathbf{L}F
.

Proof.

Let
Y
be an
X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule given by

Y(i)(B,C)=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))({}_{B}M(i),F(i)(C^{\wedge}))

for
i\in I,B\in{\mathscr{B}}(i),C\in X^{\prime}(i)
, and for each
a:i\to j
, there is
a bimodule morphism

Y(a)\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}\\
\to\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j).

(1)

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)
induces a functor
\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)\to\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}(i)
,
hence
Y(i)
is a quasi-functor. It is a quasi-equivalence if
\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F}(i))
induces an equivalence
{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)\to{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}(i)
.
For any
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(i)
, we have

\displaystyle x^{\prime\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}Y(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}\cong

\displaystyle R\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(M(i),\mathbf{F}(i)(x^{\prime\wedge}))\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)})\cong

\displaystyle R\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(M(j),\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}(i)(x^{\prime\wedge})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})\cong

\displaystyle R\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(M(j),\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}(j)((x^{\prime\wedge})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}))\cong

\displaystyle Y(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime})\cong

\displaystyle(x^{\prime\wedge})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j)

Then
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Y
induces a
1
-morphism
\underline{X^{\prime}}\to\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}
, hence
Y
is a quasi-functor, that is, we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(a)}

Note that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}M
induces an equivalence
{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{B}}
if and only if

\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}
induces an equivalence
{\mathbb{Z}}\underline{X^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathscr{U}}
. The second assertion now follows from Lemma
9.9
.

(2)

There is a canonical morphism

Q\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}M(i)\to\mathbf{L}F(i)(Q)

which is invertible for
Q\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}^{b}(X^{\prime}(i))
. It is invertible for arbitrary
Q\in{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))
if and only if
\mathbf{L}F(i)
commutes with direct sums.

(3)

If
(X^{\prime}(i),Z(i))
is a lift of
{\mathscr{U}}(i)
and
F(i)=\operatorname{-}\otimes Z(i)
, then
Y(i)
is a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}(i)\to{\mathscr{B}}(i)
and
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Y(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}M(i)\cong\mathbf{L}F(i)
by
[
28
, Lemma 7.3]
. Therefore the assertions of (3) are from (1) and (2).

∎

Recall that the Morita theory for dg categories was given by Keller.
We will give a similar theorem in our setting.

Theorem 9.17
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then we have implications
(1)\Rightarrow(2)\Rightarrow(3)\Rightarrow(4)
. If
X
is
\Bbbk
-flat, then we also have the implication
(4)\Rightarrow(1)
.

(1)

There exists an
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
such that

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.

(2)

There exists a
1
-morphism
(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
in the
2
-category

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
such that

\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon

{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
is an equivalence in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.

(3)

There exists a tilting colax functor
{\mathscr{T}}
for
X
, and
there exists a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule
E
.

(4)

There exists a tilting colax functor
{\mathscr{T}}
for
X
, and
there exist
1
-morphisms

(\mathbf{G},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{T}})\ \text{and }(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{T}})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

in the
2
-category

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
such that

\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{G},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime})\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}})\ \text{and}\ \mathbf{L}(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi})\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)

are equivalences in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).
We can take
(\mathbf{F},\boldsymbol{\psi}):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}}Z
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3).
Assume that the statement (2) holds.
Then for each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, we have a diagram

(9.22)

where note that
\boldsymbol{\psi}(a)
is a dg natural transformation by assumption.
For each
i\in I_{0}
, we set
{\mathscr{U}}(i)
to be the
full dg subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))

with

\begin{aligned} {\mathscr{U}}(i)_{0}=\{\mathbf{L}\mathbf{F}(i)(C^{\wedge})\mid C\in X^{\prime}(i)_{0}\}\end{aligned}.

Let
({\mathscr{T}},M)
be the standard lift of
{\mathscr{U}}

(see Definition
9.11
(3)):

\displaystyle{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{\mathbf{p}_{X(i)}U\mid U\in{\mathscr{U}}(i)_{0}\},

\displaystyle M(i)(A,\mathbf{p}_{X(i)}U)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{p}_{X(i)}U(A)

for all
i\in I_{0}
.
By Lemma
9.16
, we define a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule
E
by setting

E(i)(B,C)=\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i))(M(i),\mathbf{F}(i)(C^{\wedge})(B)).

for all
i\in I_{0}
,
B\in{\mathscr{T}}(i),C\in X^{\prime}(i)
, and a bimodule morphism

E(a)\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}E(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{B}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(a)}\\
\to\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}E(j).

It follows from
[
28
, Lemma 7.3]
that
E(i)
is a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}(i)\to{\mathscr{T}}(i)
, and it follows from Lemma
9.16
that we have the following diagram such that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}E(a)
is an 2-isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{T}}}(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X^{\prime}}(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\mathscr{T}}}(j).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}E(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}Y(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}E(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}E(a)}

(3)
\Rightarrow
(4).
Assume that the statement (3) holds.
Then there exists a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule
E

for
X
, which gives us the necessary 1-morphism

(\mathbf{G},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime}):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}}E\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime})\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{T}})

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCAT})
and
an equivalence
\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{G},\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\prime})=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}E\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}})

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.

We define a
{\mathscr{T}}
-
X
-bimodule
M
as follows.
For each
i\in I_{0}
, we set

{}_{B}M(i)_{A}:=B(A)\cong{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))(A^{\wedge},B)

for all
B\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0},A\in X(i)_{0}
.
For each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, we define
a
0
-cocycle morphism

M(a)\colon M(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\to\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}M(j)

of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)
-
X(j)
-bimodules by

\displaystyle M(a)_{C^{\wedge}}\colon C^{\wedge}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}M(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\cong M(i)(\operatorname{-},C)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}

\displaystyle\cong C\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\cong{\mathscr{T}}(a)(C)\cong C^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes M(j)

for all
a\in I_{1}
and
C\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)
.

Let
(\sigma,\rho)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
be an
I
-equivariant inclusion,
and
i\in I_{0}
.
We define a dg functor

F(i)\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{T}}(i))\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))

by setting
F(i):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}M(i)
.
Then since for each
B\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
, the right dg
X(i)
-module

{}_{B}M(i)=B\ (\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}\subseteq\operatorname{{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathrm{p}}}(X(i))_{0})

is homotopically projective,

F(i)
preserves homotopically projectives by Lemma
7.18
.
Now for any
B,C\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
, the bimodule
M(i)
defines a morphism

{}_{?}M(i)\colon{\mathscr{T}}(i)(B,C)\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X(i))({}_{B}M(i),{}_{C}M(i))
in
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Bbbk)
by sending each
f\colon B\to C
to
{}_{f}U(i)\colon{}_{B}M(i)\to{}_{C}M(i)
.
Here since we have
{}_{B}M(i)=B
,
{}_{f}M(i)=f
by definition,
{}_{?}M(i)
is the identity of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)(B,C)
.
Hence it induces an isomorphism in homology. Moreover,
\{{}_{B}M(i)\mid B\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}\}={\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
and
{\mathscr{T}}(i)
is a tilting dg category for
X(i)
.
Hence by
[
28
, Lemma 6.1(a)]
,

\mathbf{L}F(i)=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}M(i)\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(i))\to{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))
is a triangle equivalence. Next for each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
, we construct a
2
-morphism
\boldsymbol{\psi}(a)
in the diagram

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\psi}(a):=\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}M(a):

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

with
\mathbf{L}G(i)
and
\mathbf{L}F(i)
are triangle equivalences and

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}\phi)(a)\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(a))\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)\cong\mathbf{L}G(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}\psi)(a)\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X(a))\circ\mathbf{L}F(i)\cong\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(a))

are
2
-isomorphisms.

Let
M(i)
be the bimodule
M(i)(B,A)=G(i)(A^{\wedge})(B)
for
A\in X^{\prime}(i)
and
B\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)
, and let
N(i)
be the bimodule
N(i)(D,C)=F(i)(C^{\wedge})(D)
for
C\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)
and
B\in X(i)
. By Lemma
9.15
, we have 2-morphisms

N(a):=(N(a)_{T})_{T\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{T}}(i))}\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\Rightarrow\operatorname{-}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j)

and

M(a):=(M(a)_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X^{\prime}(i))}\colon\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}M(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\Rightarrow\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X(j)}M(j).

Then by
[
28
, Lemma 6.3(b)]
,

\mathbf{L}F(i)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)=(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}N(i))\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(i))\cong\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}(M(i)\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i))
,
where we set

\mathbf{p}N(i):=\mathbf{p}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)\mbox{-}X(i)}N(i)
for short,
which is a triangle equivalence as the composite of triangle equivalences.

We define an

X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
up to associators.
It is given as follows:

\displaystyle Z(i):

\displaystyle=M(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\mathbf{p}N(i)\text{ for all }i\in I_{0},

\displaystyle Z(a):

\displaystyle=\ {\boldsymbol{a}_{M(i),_{\mathbf{p}}N(i),\overline{X(a)}}^{-1}}\circ(M(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\mathbf{p}N(a))\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{M(i),\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)},\mathbf{p}N(j)}

\displaystyle\circ(M(a)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}\mathbf{p}N(j))\circ\boldsymbol{a}_{\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},M(j),\mathbf{p}N(j)}^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{for all $a\colon i\to j$ in $I$},

where the
X^{\prime}(i)
-
X(i)
-bimodule morphism
Z(a)
is defined by the commutative diagram

(9.23)

where the morphism
\mathbf{p}N(a)
is defined as follows:
we consider the triangle

\mathbf{p}N(i)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{N(i)}}N(i)\xrightarrow{\delta_{N(i)}}\boldsymbol{a}N(i)\to

of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)\mbox{-}X(i)
-bimodules.
By tensoring with

\overline{X(a)}
from the right, and with
\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}
from the left,
this induces two triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{a}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}\mathbf{p}N(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{p}N(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{a}N(a)}

(9.24)

of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)\mbox{-}X(j)
-bimodules.
We now show that

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{T}}(i)^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}X(j))(\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})=0.

For any
t\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
, we have isomorphisms

t^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)}\cong{\mathscr{T}}(j)(\operatorname{-},{\mathscr{T}}(a)t)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)}\cong\boldsymbol{a}N(j)(\operatorname{-},{\mathscr{T}}(a)t),

where the last term is an acyclic right dg
X(j)
-module.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(t^{\wedge}\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},t^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(t^{\wedge}\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},\boldsymbol{a}N(j)(\operatorname{-},{\mathscr{T}}(a)t))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(i)(t^{\wedge}\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i),\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\boldsymbol{a}N(j)(\operatorname{-},{\mathscr{T}}(a)t)).

Here we have
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(\overline{X(a)},\boldsymbol{a}N(j)(\operatorname{-},{\mathscr{T}}(a)t))=0

because
\overline{X(a)}
is a homotopocally projective right dg
X(j)
-module.
As a consequence, we have

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(t^{\wedge}\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},t^{\wedge}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})=0.

(9.25)

Take any
\alpha\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{T}}(i)^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}X(j))(\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})
.
Then the equality (
9.25
) means that
for any
t\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
,
we have

0=\alpha(?,t)\colon\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))((\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})(?,t),(\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})(?,t)).

Thus
\alpha=0
, and hence we have

\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}({\mathscr{T}}(j)^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}X(j))(\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}{\boldsymbol{a}N(j)})=0.

Then by the diagaram
(
9.24
), we see that
there is a unique morphism
\linebreak\mathbf{p}N(a)\colon\mathbf{p}N(i)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}\to\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}\mathbf{p}N(j)

such that the diagram (
9.24
) commutes.
In this way
\mathbf{p}N(a)
is defined.

Then by the diagram (
9.23
), the
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
is defined, and we have a
1
-morphism
\operatorname{-}\otimes_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

with the following diagram

\hbox to221.78pt{\vbox to58.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 111.53731pt\lower-29.1831pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

By Definition
2.15
, the composite
(\mathbf{L}F,\mathbf{L}\psi)\circ(\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\phi)

of
(\mathbf{L}F,\mathbf{L}\psi)
and

(\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\phi)

is a 1-morphism from
{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
to
{\mathscr{D}}(X)
defined by

(\mathbf{L}F,\mathbf{L}\psi)\circ(\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\phi):=(\mathbf{L}F\circ\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi),

where

\mathbf{L}F\circ\mathbf{L}G:=(\mathbf{L}F(i)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i))_{i\in I_{0}}
and for each
a\colon i\to j
in
I
,

(\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi)(a):=(\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ\mathbf{L}\phi(a))\bullet(\mathbf{L}\psi(a)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i))

is the pasting of the diagram

(9.26)

Therefore,

\begin{aligned} \mathbf{L}F(j)(\mathbf{L}\phi)(a)\colon\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(a))\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ\mathbf{L}G(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))\\
(\mathbf{L}\psi)(a)\mathbf{L}G(i)\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X(a))\circ\mathbf{L}F(i)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{T}}(a))\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)\\
\end{aligned}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi)(a)

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ\mathbf{L}\phi(a))\bullet(\mathbf{L}\psi(a)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i))\colon

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}(X(a))\circ\mathbf{L}F(i)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ\mathbf{L}G(j)\circ{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(a))

We show that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}Z(a)
is equivalent to
(\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi)(a)
.

From the definition of
Z(a)
, we have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}Z(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}Z(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}(M(i)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\mathbf{p}N(i))\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}(M(j)\otimes_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}\mathbf{p}N(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}(M(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}N(i))\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}(M(j)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}(N(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(j)}M(j))\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}(\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}Z(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{a}_{M(i),N(i),\overline{X(a)}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(i)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(i)}N(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{a}_{M(i),\overline{{\mathscr{T}}(a)},N(j)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(a)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{T}}(j)}N(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{a}_{\overline{X^{\prime}(a)},M(j),N(j)}^{-1}}

where
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}N(a)
and
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(a)
are 2-isomorphisms.
Note that for each
i\in I_{0}
,
we have a
2
-isomorphism

\xi_{i}\colon\mathbf{L}F(i)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)=(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}N(i))\circ(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}M(i))\overset{\cong}{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}(M(i)\otimes\mathbf{p}N(i))=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}Z(i),

we get the following commutative diagram

(9.27)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}F,\mathbf{L}\psi)\circ(\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\phi):=(\mathbf{L}F\circ\mathbf{L}G,\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\mathbf{L}F\circ\mathbf{L}G)(i),(\mathbf{L}\psi\circ\mathbf{L}\phi)(a))_{i\in I_{0},a\in I_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbf{L}G(i)\circ\mathbf{L}F(i),(\mathbf{L}F(j)\circ\mathbf{L}\phi(a))\bullet(\mathbf{L}\psi(a)\circ\mathbf{L}G(i)))_{i\in I_{0},a\in I_{1}}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:pasting-Lps-Lph}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}(i)}Z(i),\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}Z(a))_{i\in I_{0},a\in I_{1}}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:Lps-Lph-Z}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z.

Then
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
.
∎

Definition 9.18
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
The equivalence of the form
in the statement (1) in Theorem
9.17
above
(namely, of the form

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X^{\prime}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(X)
in

\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
for an
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule
Z
)
is called a
standard derived equivalence
from
X^{\prime}
to
X
, and
if it exists, then
we say that
X^{\prime}

is standardly derived equivalent to

X
.
We denote this fact by
X^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{sd}}{\leadsto}X
.
At present we do not know whether this relation is symmetric or not.
See Problem
9.20
.

Remark 9.19
.

For dg categories
{\mathscr{A}},{\mathscr{B}}
, the relation that
{\mathscr{B}}
is standardly derived equivalent to
{\mathscr{A}}

(see Definition
9.5
), is known to
be symmetric (take
Z^{\scriptsize\top}
instead of
Z
, see
[
28
, 6.2]
).

Problem 9.20
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Under which condition,

X^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{sd}}{\leadsto}X
implies
X\overset{\mathrm{sd}}{\leadsto}X^{\prime}
?

10.
Derived equivalences of Grothendieck constructions

In this section,
we give our second main result stating that
for
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
,
if
X^{\prime}
is standardly derived equivalent to
X
,
then their Grothendieck constructions are derived equivalent.

10.1.
Quasi-equivalence 1-morphisms

In this subsection, we use the contents in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Assume that
(F,\psi)\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
is a quasi-equivalence 1-morphism. Then
\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon\textstyle\int X\to\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
is a quasi-equivalence.

Proof.

Recall that a 1-morphism

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)\colon\textstyle\int X\to\textstyle\int X^{\prime}

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
is defined by

•

for each
{}_{i}x\in\textstyle\int(X)_{0}
,
\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x):={}_{i}(F(i)x)
, and

•

for each
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
and
each
f=(f_{a})_{a\in I(i,j)}\in(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)
,

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)(f):=(F(j)f_{a}\circ\psi(a)x)_{a\in I(i,j)}
, where each entry is the composite of

X^{\prime}(a)F(i)x\xrightarrow{\psi(a)x}F(j)X(a)x\xrightarrow{F(j)f_{a}}F(j)y.

Then we have the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x),\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{j}y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)x,F(j)y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\int(F,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\int(F,\psi)}

(10.28)

Assume that
(F,\psi):X\to X^{\prime}
is a quasi-equivalence, that is

(1)

For each
i\in I_{0}
,
F(i):X(i)\to X^{\prime}(i)

is a quasi-equivalence; and

(2)

For each
a\in I_{1}
,
\psi(a)
is a 2-quasi-isomorphism.

Claim 1
.

Let
{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}
.
Then the restriction

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)_{{{}_{i}x},{{}_{j}y}}\colon(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)\to(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x),\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{j}y))

of
\textstyle\int(F,\psi)
to

(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Indeed,
we have to show that for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following is an isomorphism:

H^{k}(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)\xrightarrow{H^{k}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)_{{}_{i}x,{{}_{j}y}})}H^{k}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{i}x),\textstyle\int(F,\psi)({}_{j}y)).

By (
10.28
), it is decomposed as follows:

\small\hbox to364.32pt{\vbox to148.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 182.1595pt\lower-71.92815pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(10.29)

By assumption,
H^{k}(F(j)_{X(a)x,y})
is an isomorphism for all
a\in I(i,j)
, and hence
so is
\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}H^{k}(F(j))
.
Therefore, it remains to show that

\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y))

is an isomorphism.
Let
a\colon i\to j
be a morphism in
I
.
Then since

\psi(a)\colon X^{\prime}(a)F(i)\Rightarrow F(j)X(a)

is a 2-quasi-isomorphism, by definition, we have a 2-isomorphism

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}\colon\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}\Rightarrow\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(j)X(a)}.

By specializing at
x^{\wedge}\in{\mathscr{D}}(X(i))_{0}
, this yields an isomorphism

x^{\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{\psi(a)}\colon x^{\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)F(i)}\to x^{\wedge}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{X(i)}\overline{F(j)X(a)},

i.e., an isomorphism

(\psi(a)_{x})^{\wedge}\colon(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)(x))^{\wedge}\ \raise 3.44444pt\hbox{${}^{\sim}$}\kern-6.99997pt\hbox{$\to$}\ (F(j)X(a)(x))^{\wedge}

in
{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))
.
Since we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))((F(j)X(a)(x))^{\wedge},(F(j)(y))^{\wedge}[k])}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))((X^{\prime}(a)F(i)(x))^{\wedge},(F(j)(y))^{\wedge}[k])}
\textstyle{H^{k}X^{\prime}(j)(F(j)X(a)(x),F(j)(y))}
\textstyle{H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)(x),F(j)y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime}(j))((\psi(a)_{x})^{\wedge},(F(j)(y))^{\wedge}[k])}
\scriptstyle{H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y))}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

with the vertical canonical isomorphisms,
we see that

\begin{split}H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y))\colon H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(F(j)X(a)(x),F(j)(y))\\
\to H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)(x),F(j)y)\end{split}

is an isomorphism, and hence so is

\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}H^{k}(X^{\prime}(j)(\psi(a)_{x},F(j)y))
, as desired.
Therefore, we conclude that

H^{k}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)_{{}_{i}x,{{}_{j}y}})
is an isomorphim
by the commutative diagram (
10.29
).
Hence it follows that

\textstyle\int(F,\psi)_{{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y}
is a quasi-isomorphism for all
{}_{i}x
and
{}_{j}y
.

Next we show the following:

Claim 2
.

H^{0}(\textstyle\int X)\xrightarrow{H^{0}((\textstyle\int(F,\psi))}H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})

is an equivalence.

By Claim 1 for
k=0
, we have that

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}H^{0}(X(j)(X(a)x,y))\xrightarrow{H^{0}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi)_{{}_{i}x,{}_{j}y})}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}H^{0}((X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)F(i)x,F(j)y))

is bijective for all
{}_{i}x
and
{}_{j}y
. Thus,

H^{0}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi))\colon H^{0}(\textstyle\int X)\to H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})

is fully faithful.
It only remains to show that it is dense.
By the definition of Grothendieck construction, we have

H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})_{0}=H^{0}(\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{0}}X^{\prime}(i)_{0})=\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{0}}H^{0}(X^{\prime}(i))_{0}=\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{0}}X^{\prime}(i)_{0}.

For any
{}_{i}x^{\prime}\in\bigsqcup_{i\in I_{0}}X^{\prime}(i)_{0}
with
i\in I_{0}
and
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(i)_{0}
,
note that

H^{0}(X(i))\xrightarrow{H^{0}(F(i))}H^{0}(X^{\prime}(i))

is dense by (1) above. Thus there exists
x\in X(i)_{0}
such that
y:=F(i)(x)=H^{0}(F(i)(x))\cong x^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(X^{\prime}(i))
.
Thus there exists
f:x^{\prime}\ \raise 3.44444pt\hbox{${}^{\sim}$}\kern-6.99997pt\hbox{$\to$}\ y
in
H^{0}(X^{\prime}(i))
.
Since

H^{0}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi))(_{i}x)=\textstyle\int(F,\psi)(_{i}x)={}_{i}F(i)(x)={}_{i}y,

it suffices to show that
{}_{i}y\cong{}_{i}x^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\textstyle\int(X^{\prime}))
.
Noting that

\displaystyle H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}x^{\prime},{}_{i}y)

\displaystyle=H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}x^{\prime},{}_{i}y))=H^{0}(\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}(X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime},y))

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}H^{0}((X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime},y)),\text{ and}

\displaystyle H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}y,{}_{i}x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}y,{}_{i}x^{\prime}))=H^{0}(\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}(X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)y,x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}H^{0}((X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)y,x^{\prime})),

we can take elements

\displaystyle(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f^{-1}\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(y))_{b\in I(i,i)}

\displaystyle\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}H^{0}((X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)y,x^{\prime})),\text{and}

\displaystyle(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(x))_{a\in I(i,i)}

\displaystyle\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i)}H^{0}((X^{\prime}(i)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime},y)),

where entries are of the following forms

\displaystyle X^{\prime}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})y\xrightarrow{X^{\prime}_{i}(y)}y\xrightarrow{f^{-1}}x^{\prime},\quad X^{\prime}(1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i})x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{X^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\prime})}x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}y,

respectively.
A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f^{-1}\circ X_{i}^{\prime}(y))_{b\in I(i,i)}\circ(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\prime}))_{a\in I(i,i)}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}x^{\prime}},

\displaystyle(\delta_{a,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\prime}))_{a\in I(i,i)}\circ(\delta_{b,1\kern-2.04861pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{i}}f^{-1}\circ X^{\prime}_{i}(y))_{b\in I(i,i)}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{}_{i}y}

Then we have
{}_{i}y\cong{}_{i}x^{\prime}
in
H^{0}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})
.
Therefore
H^{0}(\textstyle\int(F,\psi))
is dense.
∎

The following is the main result in this subsection.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Assume that
X
is
\Bbbk
-flat. If there exists a tilting colax functor
{\mathscr{T}}
for
X

and there exists a quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism from
X^{\prime}
to
{\mathscr{T}}
,
then
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
is derived equivalent to
\textstyle\int X
.

Proof.

Note that
\textstyle\int(X)
is also
\Bbbk
-flat by definition of
\textstyle\int(X)
.
Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be a tilting colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

with an
I
-equivariant inclusion

(\sigma,\rho)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
.
Put
(P,\phi):=(P_{X},\phi_{X})
for short.
Let
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
be the full dg subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\textstyle\int X)

consisting of the objects
\operatorname{per}(P(i))(U)
(
\in\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X)
)
with
i\in I_{0}
and
U\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
, which is called the

gluing
of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)
’s.

We now show that
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
is a tilting dg subcategory for
\textstyle\int(X)
. By Proposition
7.31
, the objects in
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}(i)
are homotopically projective for all
i\in I_{0}
.
For each
i\in I_{0}
and
x\in X(i)
,
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\cong X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{P(i)}

\displaystyle=X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

\displaystyle\cong(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(x))=(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x).

Thus

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{per}(P(i))(\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}(i))

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}\{\operatorname{per}(P(i))(U)\mid U\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}.

Therefore,
\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X)
, and hence

{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
is a tilting dg subcategory for
\textstyle\int X
, as desired.
In particular, we see that

\textstyle\int X
is derived equivalent to
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
.
Let
(F,\psi)
be the restriction of
\operatorname{per}((P,\phi))
to
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Then by construction
(F,\psi)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\to\Delta({\mathscr{T}}^{\prime})

is a dense functor, and it is an
I
-precovering because
so is

\operatorname{per}((P,\phi))\colon\operatorname{per}(X)\to\Delta(\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X))

by Proposition
7.30
.
Thus
(F,\psi)
is an
I
-covering, which shows that

{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}\simeq\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
by Corollary
6.3
.
Thus we have

\textstyle\int X\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}.

(10.30)

Since there exists a quasi-equivalence from
X^{\prime}
to
{\mathscr{T}}

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
, we have a quasi-equivalence from

\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
to

\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}\ (\simeq{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime})
in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
by Proposition
10.1
, and hence
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
and
\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
are derived equivalent by
Theorem
A.1
.
As a consequence,
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
is derived equivalent to
\textstyle\int X
.
∎

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Since the relation that
X^{\prime}
is quasi-equivalent to
X
is
not symmetric with respect to
X^{\prime}
and
X
,
we consider a zigzag chain of quasi-equivalences between them
defined as follows.

Definition 10.3
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then a
zigzag chain of quasi-equivalence
1
-morphisms
between
X
and
X^{\prime}
is a chain
of 1-morphisms of the form

X=:X_{0}\xleftarrow{(F_{1},\psi_{1})}X_{1}\xrightarrow{(F_{2},\psi_{2})}\cdots\xleftarrow{(F_{n-1},\psi_{n-1})}X_{n-1}\xrightarrow{(F_{n},\psi_{n})}X_{n}:=X^{\prime}

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
with
n
even
\geq 2
, where
(F_{i},\psi_{i})
are quasi-equivalence
1
-morphisms for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Note that a quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism
X\xrightarrow{(F_{2},\psi_{2})}X^{\prime}
is also regarded
as a zigzag chain of quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism
by setting
n=2
and
(F_{1},\psi_{1})
to be the identity 1-morphism.

The following is immediate from
Proposition
10.5
,
Theorems
A.1
and
10.2
.

Corollary 10.4
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Assume that
X
is
\Bbbk
-flat and that there exists a tilting colax functor
{\mathscr{T}}
for
X

such that
there exists a zigzag chain of quasi-equivalence 1-morphisms
between
X^{\prime}
and
{\mathscr{T}}
in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Then
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
and
\textstyle\int X
are derived equivalent.

Proof.

By assumption, we have a zigzag chain of quasi-equivalences between
X^{\prime}
and
{\mathscr{T}}
of the form

{\mathscr{T}}=:X_{0}\xleftarrow{(F_{1},\psi_{1})}X_{1}\xrightarrow{(F_{2},\psi_{2})}\cdots\xleftarrow{(F_{n-1},\psi_{n-1})}X_{n-1}\xrightarrow{(F_{n},\psi_{n})}X_{n}:=X^{\prime}

in
\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
with
n
even
\geq 2
, where
(F_{i},\psi_{i})
are quasi-equivalences for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Then by Proposition
10.1
, we have a zigzag chain

\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}=:\textstyle\int X_{0}\xleftarrow{}\textstyle\int X_{1}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xleftarrow{}\textstyle\int X_{n-1}\xrightarrow{}\textstyle\int X_{n}:=\textstyle\int X^{\prime}

of quasi-equivalences of dg categories, and hence a sequence of derived equivalences

\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}=:\textstyle\int X_{0}\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int X_{1}\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\cdots\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int X_{n-1}\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int X_{n}:=\textstyle\int X^{\prime}

by Theorem
A.1
.
Since to be derived equivalent is an equivalence relation,
we have
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
.
It follows from (the proof of) Theorem
10.2

(see (
10.32
)) that
\textstyle\int X\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
.
Therefore,
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
and
\textstyle\int X
are derived equivalent.
∎

10.2.
Quasi-equivalence bimodules

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Assume that there exists a quasi-equivalence
X^{\prime}
-
X
-bimodule

E
. Then we have a quasi-equivalence
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
-
\textstyle\int X
-bimodule

\textstyle\int E
.

Proof.

We construct a
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
-
\textstyle\int X
-bimodule
\textstyle\int E
, namely a dg functor

\textstyle\int E\colon(\textstyle\int X)^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{\Bbbk}(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})\to\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk).

as follows.

On objects.

Let
({}_{i}x,{}_{i^{\prime}}x^{\prime})\in(\textstyle\int X)_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})_{0}
, where

i,i^{\prime}\in I_{0},\ x\in X(i)_{0},\ x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(i^{\prime})_{0}
.
Then we set

(\textstyle\int E)({}_{i}x,{}_{i^{\prime}}x^{\prime}):=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i^{\prime})}E(i^{\prime})(X(a)x,x^{\prime})\in\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\Bbbk)_{0}.

On morphisms.

Let
f\in\textstyle\int(X)^{\mathrm{op}}({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)=\textstyle\int(X)({}_{j}y,{}_{i}x)=\bigoplus_{c\in I(j,i)}X(i)(X(c)y,x)

and
g\in\textstyle\int(X^{\prime})({}_{i^{\prime}}x^{\prime},{}_{j^{\prime}}y^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{d\in I(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}X(j^{\prime})(X^{\prime}(d)x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
Then by noting that
f_{c}\in X(j)(X(c)y,x)
and
g_{d}\in X(j^{\prime})(X^{\prime}(d)x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

for all
c\in I(j,i),d\in I(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
,
we define
(\textstyle\int E)(f\otimes g)
as follows (see the diagram below).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int E)({}_{i}x,{}_{i^{\prime}}x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int E)({}_{j}y,{}_{j^{\prime}}y^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i^{\prime})}E(i^{\prime})(X(a)x,x^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{b\in I(j,j^{\prime})}E(j^{\prime})(X(b)y,y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textstyle\int E)(f\otimes g)}

Let
(v_{a})_{a\in I(i,i^{\prime})}\in\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,i^{\prime})}E(i^{\prime})(X(a)x,x^{\prime})
,
where for each
a\in I(i,i^{\prime})
, we have
v_{a}\in E(i^{\prime})(X(a)x,x^{\prime})
.
Here in
I
, we have the following setting:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

We then set

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int E)(f\otimes g)((v_{a})_{a\in I(i,i^{\prime})})

\displaystyle:=\left(\sum_{b=dac}((X(d)\circ X_{ac})\bullet X_{d,ac})_{y}\cdot g_{d}\cdot E(d)(v_{a})\cdot X(d)X(a)(f_{c})\right)_{b\in I(j,j^{\prime})},

where the sum is taken over all triples
(c,a,d)\in I(j,i)\times I(i,i^{\prime})\times I(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

such that
b=dac
.
The right half of the terms in the summation can be visualized
in the following diagram, where the dashed arrows stand for “elements” of
E
:

\hbox to386.42pt{\vbox to58.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 193.20598pt\lower-29.1831pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

and the left half in the following:

X(b)=X(dac)\xrightarrow{(X_{d,ac})_{y}}X(d)X(ac)\xrightarrow{X(d)\circ(X_{ac})_{y}}X(d)X(a)X(c).

Note that
E(d)\colon E(i^{\prime})\to E(j^{\prime})
is an
X^{\prime}(i^{\prime})
-
X(i^{\prime})
-bimodule morphism,
where the
X^{\prime}(i^{\prime})
-
X(i^{\prime})
-bimodule structure of
E(j^{\prime})
is defined from
its
X^{\prime}(j^{\prime})
-
X(j^{\prime})
-bimodule structure
by using the functors
X(d)\colon X(i^{\prime})\to X(j^{\prime})

and
X^{\prime}(d)\colon X^{\prime}(i^{\prime})\linebreak\to X^{\prime}(j^{\prime})
, which explains the vertical correspondence in
the diagram above.

We next show that
\textstyle\int E
is a quasi-equivalence bimodule.
We have to show the conditions (a) and (b) in Definition

9.4
.
We show the condition (a) by using Lemma
9.3
.
Consider the canonical morphism

(P,\phi)\colon X\to\Delta(\textstyle\int X)
.
Then
for each
i\in I_{0}
and
x\in X(i)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\cong X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{P(i)}

\displaystyle=X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

\displaystyle\cong(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(x))=(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x).

For each
i\in I_{0}
and
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}(i)
,
there exists some
x\in X(i)_{0}
such that

E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\cong X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{per}(P(i))(E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\cong E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{P(i)}

\displaystyle=E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

\displaystyle\cong X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

\displaystyle\cong(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(x))=(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x).

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int E)({}_{?}\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{a\in I(?,i)}E(i)(X(a)(\operatorname{-}),x^{\prime})

(10.31)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(?,i)}X(i)(X(a)(\operatorname{-}),x)=(\textstyle\int X)({}_{?}\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x).

Thus

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{per}(P(i))(\operatorname{thick}E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}\{\operatorname{per}(P(i))E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}(\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x^{\prime}).

Therefore,
\operatorname{thick}(\textstyle\int E)=\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X)
.
Namely, the conditions (1) and (3) in Lemma
9.3
hold.
It remains to show the condition (2) of this lemma,
that is, to show that

(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}x^{\prime},{}_{j}y^{\prime})\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{\textstyle\int X}((\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x^{\prime}),(\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{j}y^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism.

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{\textstyle\int X}((\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x^{\prime}),(\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{j}y^{\prime}))\cong\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(\textstyle\int X)((\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x),(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{j}y))

\displaystyle\cong(\textstyle\int X)({}_{i}x,{}_{j}y)=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X(j)(X(a)x,y)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(X(j)(\operatorname{-},X(a)x),X(j)(\operatorname{-},y)))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},X(j)(\operatorname{-},y)))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))((E(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{X(a)},E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(X^{\prime}(i)(\operatorname{-},x^{\prime})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(i)}\overline{X^{\prime}(a)}\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}E(j),E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))(X^{\prime}(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime})\otimes_{X^{\prime}(j)}E(j),E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))((E(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime}),E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime}))).

See the diagram for the last three lines:
