Since

X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{qis}}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))((E(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime}),E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism induced by the quasi-equivalence bimodule
E
,
it follows that

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int X^{\prime})({}_{i}x^{\prime},{}_{j}y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}X^{\prime}(j)(X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{qis}}\bigoplus_{a\in I(i,j)}\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}(X(j))((E(j)(\operatorname{-},X^{\prime}(a)x^{\prime}),E(j)(\operatorname{-},y^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{\textstyle\int X}((\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x^{\prime}),(\textstyle\int E)(\operatorname{-},{}_{j}y^{\prime}))

is a quasi-isomorphism.
By Lemma
9.3
,
\textstyle\int E
induces a derived equivalence between
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
and
\textstyle\int X
.
Note that the condition (b) is trivially satisfied by (
10.31
).
Hence
\textstyle\int E
is a quasi-equivalence bimodule.
∎

The following is our second main result in this paper.

Theorem 10.6
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat})
.
Assume that
X
is
\Bbbk
-flat.
If there exist a
tilting colax functor
{\mathscr{T}}
for
X
and a quasi-equivalence

X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule (namely, if
X^{\prime}
is standardly derived
equivalent to
X
by Theorem
9.17
),
then
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
is derived equivalent to
\textstyle\int X
.

Proof.

Note that
\textstyle\int X
is also
\Bbbk
-flat by definition of
\textstyle\int X
.
Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be a tilting colax subfunctor of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)

with an
I
-equivariant inclusion

(\sigma,\rho)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\hookrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(X)
.
Put
(P,\phi):=(P_{X},\phi_{X})
for short.
Let
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
be the full dg subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\textstyle\int X)

consisting of the objects
\operatorname{per}(P(i))(U)
(
\in\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X)
)
with
i\in I_{0}
and
U\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)_{0}
, which is called the

gluing
of
{\mathscr{T}}(i)
’s.

We now show that
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
is a tilting dg subcategory for
\textstyle\int X
. By Proposition
7.31
, the objects in
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}(i)
are homotopically projective for all
i\in I_{0}
.
For each
i\in I_{0}
and
x\in X(i)
,
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\cong X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}\overline{P(i)}

\displaystyle=X(i)(\operatorname{-},x)\otimes_{X(i)}(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(?))

\displaystyle\cong(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},P(i)(x))=(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x).

Thus

\displaystyle(\textstyle\int X)(\operatorname{-},{}_{i}x)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{per}(P(i))(X(i)(\operatorname{-},x))

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{per}(P(i))(\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}(i))

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}\{\operatorname{per}(P(i))(U)\mid U\in{\mathscr{T}}(i)\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}.

Therefore,
\operatorname{thick}{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}=\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X)
, and hence

{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
is a tilting dg subcategory for
\textstyle\int X
, as desired.
In particular, we see that

\textstyle\int X
is derived equivalent to
{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}
.
Let
(F,\psi)
be the restriction of
\operatorname{per}((P,\phi))
to
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Then by construction
(F,\psi)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\to\Delta({\mathscr{T}}^{\prime})

is a dense functor, and it is an
I
-precovering because
so is

\operatorname{per}((P,\phi))\colon\operatorname{per}(X)\to\Delta(\operatorname{per}(\textstyle\int X))

by Proposition
7.30
.
Thus
(F,\psi)
is an
I
-covering, which shows that

{\mathscr{T}}^{\prime}\simeq\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
by Corollary
6.3
.
Thus we have

\textstyle\int X\overset{\text{der}}{\sim}\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}.

(10.32)

Since there exists a quasi-equivalence

X^{\prime}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule
E
, we have a quasi-equivalence

\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
-
\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule

\textstyle\int E
by Proposition
10.5
, and hence
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
and
\textstyle\int{\mathscr{T}}
are derived equivalent
(see Definition
9.7
).
As a consequence,
\textstyle\int X^{\prime}
is derived equivalent to
\textstyle\int X
.
∎

10.3.
Group actions

In the special case that
I=G
is a group, which has a unique object
*
, Theorems
10.2
and
10.6
have the forms below.

Definition 10.7
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories with
G
-actions.

(1)

A tilting dg subcategory
{\mathscr{T}}
for
{\mathscr{A}}

is called
G
-
equivariant
if
there exists a
G
-equivariant inclusion

(\sigma,\rho)\colon{\mathscr{T}}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

(2)

An
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule
Z
is called a
quasi-equivalence
if (a) the functor

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}Z\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})

is an equivalence in

\operatorname{Colax}(G,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
, and (b) it gives rise to a equivalence
\underline{{\mathscr{A}}}\to\underline{{\mathscr{B}}}
.

Corollary 10.8
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories with
G
-actions. Assume that
{\mathscr{A}}
is
\Bbbk
-flat. If there exist
a
G
-equivariant tilting dg subcategory
{\mathscr{T}}
for
{\mathscr{A}}
, and quasi-equivalence
1
-morphism from
{\mathscr{B}}\to{\mathscr{T}}
,
then the orbit categories
{\mathscr{A}}/G
and
{\mathscr{B}}/G
are derived equivalent.

This corollary will be applied in Example
11.8
.

Corollary 10.9
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories with
G
-actions. Assume that
{\mathscr{A}}
is
\Bbbk
-flat. If there exist
a
G
-equivariant tilting dg subcategory
{\mathscr{T}}
for
{\mathscr{A}}
, and a quasi-equivalence
{\mathscr{B}}
-
{\mathscr{T}}
-bimodule,
then the orbit categories

{\mathscr{A}}/G
and
{\mathscr{B}}/G
are derived equivalent.

Remark 10.10
.

Remark
9.8
also proves
Corollary
10.8
by Corollary
10.9
.

10.4.
Diagonal functors

The following is easy to verify.

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}},{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}
be in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
.
If
{\mathscr{C}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}
are standardly derived equivalent,
then so are
\Delta({\mathscr{C}})
and
\Delta({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}
is standardly derived equivalent to
{\mathscr{C}}
,
there exists a dg functor
H:{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}({\mathscr{C}})
such that
\mathbf{L}H:{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}})
is an equivalence.
Then

\Delta(\mathbf{L}H):\Delta({\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}))\to\Delta({\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{C}}))
is an equivalence,
and it yields an equivalence

\mathbf{L}\Delta(H):{\mathscr{D}}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}))\to{\mathscr{D}}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))
, where

\Delta(H):{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}))\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Delta({\mathscr{C}}))

is a dg functor.
∎

Theorem
10.2
together with the lemma above
and Example
5.2
gives us
a unified proof of the following fact.

Theorem 10.12
.

Assume that
\Bbbk
is a field and that dg
\Bbbk
-algebras
A
and
A^{\prime}
are derived equivalent.
Then the following pairs are derived equivalent as well:

(1)

dg path categories
AQ
and
A^{\prime}Q
for any quiver
Q
;

(2)

incidence dg categories
AS
and
A^{\prime}S
for any poset
S
; and

(3)

monoid dg algebras
AG
and
A^{\prime}G
for any monoid
G
.

Proof.

Assume that
A
is derived equivalent to
A^{\prime}
.
Then
A^{\prime}
is standardly derived equivalent to
A
.
By Lemma
10.11
,
\Delta(A^{\prime})
is standardly derived equivalent
to
\Delta(A)
.
Hence by Theorem
10.2
,
\Delta(A^{\prime})
and
\Delta(A)
are derived equivalent.
∎

Remark 10.13
.

We remark that for a colax functor
X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
,
the following does not hold in general:

(*)
{\mathscr{D}}(\textstyle\int X)
is equivalent to
\textstyle\int{\mathscr{D}}X

as triangulated categories.

If this would be true, then
it immediately follows that
if
{\mathscr{D}}(X)
and
{\mathscr{D}}(X^{\prime})
are equivalent, then

{\mathscr{D}}(\textstyle\int(X))
and
{\mathscr{D}}(\textstyle\int(X^{\prime}))
are equivalent.
Thus, our main theorem becomes trivial.

However, in many cases, (*) even does not have sense.
Since
\textstyle\int X\in\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}_{0}
, the left hand side
{\mathscr{D}}(\textstyle\int X)
is a triangulated category.
On the other hand,
since
{\mathscr{D}}X\in\operatorname{Colax}(I,\Bbbk\text{-}\underline{\mathbf{TRI}}^{2})
,
we do not know how to define the right hand side
\textstyle\int{\mathscr{D}}X

as a triangulated category.
For example,
let
I
be a free category defined by the quiver
1\to 2
,

A
a dg algebra regared as a dg category with only one object, and

\Delta(A)\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
the diagonal functor of
A
.
Then by Example
5.2
,
\textstyle\int\Delta(A)
is isomorphic to
the lower triagular matrix dg algebra of
A
, or equivalently, the morphism category
\operatorname{Mor}(A)
of the category
A
.
If (*) is true, then the following holds:

(#)
{\mathscr{D}}(\operatorname{Mor}(A))
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Mor}({\mathscr{D}}(A))

as categories.

Indeed,
since
{\mathscr{D}}(\Delta(A))\cong\Delta({\mathscr{D}}(A))

(i.e., the derived tensor product by the
A
-
A
-bimodule
A

is isomorphic to the identity of
{\mathscr{D}}(A)
),
we heve

{\mathscr{D}}(\operatorname{Mor}(A))\cong{\mathscr{D}}(\textstyle\int(\Delta(A)))
is equivalent (by (*)) to

\textstyle\int{\mathscr{D}}(\Delta(A))\cong\textstyle\int\Delta({\mathscr{D}}(A))\cong\operatorname{Mor}{\mathscr{D}}(A)
.

We do not know how to define a triangulated category structure on
\operatorname{Mor}({\mathscr{D}}(A))
.
Even in the case that it was possible,
as is easily seen, (#) does not hold even for
A=\Bbbk
,
the base field concentrated in degree
0
.

11.
Examples

In this section,
we give two examples that illustrate our main theorem.

Remark 11.1
.

Let
G
be a group, which we regard as a groupoid with only one object
*
.
Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potentials.
Regard the complete Ginzburg dg algebra
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
as a dg category with only one object,
and a
G
-action on it as a functor
X_{Q,W}\colon G\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
with

X_{Q,W}(*)=\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
.
Then

\textstyle\int(X_{Q,W})
is nothing but the orbit category
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)/G
, which is
also equivalent to the skew group dg algebra
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)*G
, and is calculated as
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q_{G},W_{G})
up to Morita equivaleces
in the case that
G
is a finite group
in
[
34
]
(see also
[
23
]
for the finite abelian case).
Therefore in this case note that
\textstyle\int(X_{Q,W})
is calculated as
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q_{G},W_{G})

up to Morita equivalences.

11.1.
Mutations, the complete Ginzburg dg algebras and derived equivalences

In the example below we will use the constructions of mutations and the Ginzburg dg algebras,
and a “tilting” bimodule given by Keller–Yang.
To make it easy to understand these examples,
we recall these constructions and fix our notations.

11.1.1.
Mutations

Let
Q
be a quiver.
A path in
Q
is said to be
cyclic
if its source and target coincide.
A potential on
Q
is an element of the closure
\operatorname{Pot}(\Bbbk Q)
of the subspace
of
\Bbbk Q
generated by all non-trivial cyclic paths in
Q
.
We say that two potentials are
cyclically equivalent
if their difference is in the closure of the subspace generated by the differences
a_{1}\cdots a_{s}-a_{2}\cdots a_{s}a_{1}
for all cycles
a_{1}\cdots a_{s}
in
Q
.

The complete path algebra
\widehat{\Bbbk Q}
is the completion of the path algebra
\Bbbk Q
with respect to the ideal generated by the arrows of
Q
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the ideal of
\widehat{\Bbbk Q}
generated by the arrows of
Q
.
A
quiver with potential
is a pair
(Q,W)
of a quiver
Q
and a potential
W
of
Q
such that
W
is in
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
and no two cyclically equivalent cyclic paths appear in the decomposition of
W
.

A quiver with potential is called
trivial
if its potential is a linear combination of cyclic paths of length
2
and its Jacobian algebra is the product of copies of the base field
\Bbbk
. A quiver with potential is called
reduced
if
\partial_{a}W
is contained in
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
for all arrows
a
of
Q
.

Let
(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
and
(Q^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime})
be two quivers with potentials such that
Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}
have the same set of vertices. Their direct sum, denoted by
(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})\oplus(Q^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime})
, is the new quiver with potential
(Q,W)
, where
Q
is the quiver whose vertex set is the same as the vertex set of
Q^{\prime}
(and
Q^{\prime\prime}
) and whose arrow set is the disjoint union of the arrow set of
Q^{\prime}
and the arrow set of
Q^{\prime\prime}
, and
W=W^{\prime}+W^{\prime\prime}
.

Two quivers with potentials
(Q,W)
and
(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
are
right-equivalent
if
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
have the same set of vertices and there exists an algebra isomorphism
\phi:\Bbbk Q\to\Bbbk Q^{\prime}
whose restriction on vertices is the identity map and
\phi(W)
and
W^{\prime}
are cyclically equivalent. Such an isomorphism
\phi
is called a right-equivalence.

For any quiver with potential
(Q,W)
, there exist a trivial quiver
with potential
(Q_{\mathrm{tri}},W_{\mathrm{tri}})
and a reduced quiver with potential
(Q_{\mathrm{red}},W_{\mathrm{red}})
such that

(Q,W)
is right-equivalent to the direct sum
(Q_{\mathrm{tri}},W_{\mathrm{tri}})\oplus(Q_{\mathrm{red}},W_{\mathrm{red}})
. Furthermore, the right-equivalence class of each of
(Q_{\mathrm{tri}},W_{\mathrm{tri}})
and
(Q_{\mathrm{red}},W_{\mathrm{red}})
is uniquely determined by the right equivalence class of
(Q,W)
. We call
(Q_{\mathrm{tri}},W_{\mathrm{tri}})
and
(Q_{\mathrm{red}},W_{\mathrm{red}})
the
trivial part
and the
reduced part
of
(Q,W)
, respectively.

Definition 11.2
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential, and
i
a vertex of
Q
. Assume the following conditions:

(1)

the quiver
Q
has no loops;

(2)

the quiver
Q
does not have
2
-cycles at
i
;

(3)

no cyclic path occurring in the expansion of
W
starts and ends at
i
.

Note that under the condition (1), any potential is cyclically equivalent to a potential satisfying (3). We define a new quiver with potential
\widetilde{\mu_{i}}(Q,W)=(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
as follows. The new
quiver
Q^{\prime}
is obtained from
Q
by the following procedure:

Step 1:

For each arrow
\beta
with target
i
and each arrow
\alpha
with source
i
, add a new arrow
[\alpha\beta]
from the source of
\beta
to the target of
\alpha
.

Step 2:

Replace each arrow
\alpha
with source or target
i
with an arrow
\alpha^{*}
in the opposite direction.

The new potential
W^{\prime}
is the sum of two potentials
W_{1}^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}_{2}
, where the potential
W_{1}^{\prime}
is obtained from
W
by replacing each composition
\alpha\beta
by
[\alpha\beta]
, where
\beta
is an arrow with target
i
, and the potential
W^{\prime}_{2}
is given by

W^{\prime}_{2}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in Q_{1}}[\alpha\beta]\beta^{*}\alpha^{*},

where the sum ranges over all pairs of arrows
\alpha
and
\beta
such that
\beta
ends at
i
and
\alpha
starts at
i
. It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\mu_{i}}(Q,W)
satisfies (1), (2) and (3).
We define
\mu_{i}(Q,W)
as the reduced part of
\widetilde{\mu_{i}}(Q,W)
, and call
\mu_{i}
the
mutation
at the vertex
i
.

11.1.2.
The complete Ginzburg dg algebras

Definition 11.3
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential. The
complete Ginzburg dg algebra

\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
is constructed as follows
[
21
]
:
Let
\widetilde{Q}
be the graded quiver with the same vertices as
Q
and whose arrows are

•

the arrows of
Q
(they all have degree
0
),

•

an arrow
\overline{\alpha}:j\to i
of degree
-1
for each arrow
\alpha:i\to j
of
Q
,

•

a loop
t_{i}:i\to i
of degree
-2
for each vertex
i
of
Q
.

The underlying graded algebra of
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
is the completion of the graded path algebra
k\widetilde{Q}
in the category of graded vector spaces with respect to the ideal generated by the arrows of
\widetilde{Q}
. Thus,
the
n
-th component of
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
consists of elements of the form
\sum_{p}\lambda_{p}p
with
\lambda_{p}\in\Bbbk
, where
p
runs over all paths of degree
n
. The differential of
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
is the unique continuous linear endomorphism homogeneous of degree
1
which satisfies the Leibniz rule

d(uv)=d(u)v+(-1)^{p}ud(v),

for all homogeneous
u
of degree
p
and all
v
, and takes the following values on the arrows of
\widetilde{Q}
:

•

da=0
for each arrow
a
of
Q
,

•

d(\overline{a})=\partial_{a}W
for each arrow
\overline{a}
of
Q
,

•

d(t_{i})=e_{i}(\sum_{a}[a,\overline{a}])e_{i}
for each vertex
i
of
Q
, where
e_{i}
is the trivial path at
i
and the sum is taken over the set of arrows of
Q
.

Remark 11.4
.

We regard the complete Ginzburg dg algebra

\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
as a dg category as follows.

•

The objects are the vertices of
\widetilde{Q}
(namely the vertices of
Q
).

•

\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)(i,j):=e_{j}\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)e_{i}
for all objects
i,j
.

•

The composition is given by the multiplication of
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
.

•

The grading and the differential are naturally defined from those of
the dg algebra structure.

The following lemma is an easy consequence of the definition
(cf.
[
31
, Lemma 2.8]
).

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential. Then the Jacobian algebra
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
is the
0
-th cohomology of the complete Ginzburg dg algebra
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
, i.e.

\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)=H^{0}(\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)).

11.1.3.
Derived equivalences

Let
(Q,W)
be a quiver with potential and
i
a fixed vertex of
Q
. We assume
(1),(2)
and
(3)

as above. Write
\widetilde{\mu_{i}}(Q,W)=(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
. Let
\Gamma=\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
be the
complete Ginzburg dg algebras associated to
(Q,W)
and
(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
, respectively.
We set
P_{j}=e_{j}\Gamma
and
P^{\prime}_{j}=e_{j}\Gamma^{\prime}
for all vertices
j
of
Q
.

We cite the following from
[
31
, Theorem 3.2]
without a proof.

Theorem 11.6
.

There is a triangle equivalence

F:{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)

which sends the
P^{\prime}_{j}
to
P_{j}
for
j\neq i
, and sends
P^{\prime}_{i}

to the cone
T_{i}
over the morphism

\displaystyle P_{i}\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q_{1},s(\alpha)=i}P_{t(\alpha)}

\displaystyle a\mapsto\sum_{\alpha\in Q_{1},s(\alpha)=i}e_{t(\alpha)}\alpha a,

The functor
F
restricts
to triangle equivalences from
\operatorname{per}(\Gamma^{\prime})
to
\operatorname{per}(\Gamma)
and from
{\mathscr{D}}_{fd}(\Gamma^{\prime})
to
{\mathscr{D}}_{fd}(\Gamma)
.

The proof is based on a construction of a
\Gamma^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-bimodule
T
,
and
F
is defined by

F:=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma}T\colon{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{\prime})\to{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)
.
We recall the construction of
T
by Keller-Yang below.
As a right
\Gamma
-module, let
T
be the direct sum of
T_{i}
and
P_{j}
for all
j\in Q_{0}
with
j\neq i
.
A left
\Gamma^{\prime}
-module structure on
T
will be defined in the next proposition.
To this end we define a map

f\colon\{e_{j}\mid j\in Q_{0}\}\cup(\widetilde{Q^{\prime}})_{1}\to\operatorname{End}_{\Gamma}(T)

as follows.
First, we set
f(e_{j}):=f_{j}:T_{j}\to T_{j}
to be the identity map for all
j\in Q_{0}
.

We denote by
\lambda_{a}
the left multiplication
x\mapsto ax
by
a
below when this makes sense, and
by
e_{\Sigma i}
the unique idempotent in
\Gamma
such that

e_{\Sigma i}\Gamma=\Sigma P_{i}=P_{i}[1]
, the shift of
P_{i}
, for all
i\in Q_{0}
.

Let
\alpha\in Q_{1}
with
s(\alpha)=i
.
Then define

f_{\alpha^{\ast}}:T_{t(\alpha)}\to T_{i}
of degree
0
as
the cannonical embedding

T_{t(\alpha)}=P_{t(\alpha)}\hookrightarrow T_{i}
, that is,

f_{\alpha^{\ast}}:=\lambda_{e_{t}(\alpha)}:T_{t(\alpha)}\to T_{i},\quad a\mapsto e_{t(\alpha)}a.

Define also the morphsim
f_{\overline{\alpha^{\ast}}}:T_{i}\to T_{t(\alpha)}
of degree
-1
by

f_{\overline{\alpha^{\ast}}}((e_{\Sigma i})a_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}e_{t(\rho)}a_{\rho})=-\alpha t_{i}a_{i}-\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}\alpha\overline{\rho}a_{\rho}

Let
\beta\in Q_{1}
with
t(\beta)=i
.
Then define the morphism
f_{\beta^{\ast}}:T_{i}\to T_{s(\beta)}
of degree
0
by

f_{\beta^{\ast}}((e_{\Sigma i})a_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}e_{t(\rho)}a_{\rho})=-\overline{\beta}a_{i}-\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}(\partial_{\rho\beta}W)a_{\rho}.

Define also the morphsim
f_{\overline{\beta^{\ast}}}:T_{s(\beta)}\to T_{i}
of degree
-1
as the composite of the morphism
\lambda_{e_{\Sigma i}\beta}\colon T_{s(\beta)}\to\Sigma P_{i}
and the cannonical embedding
\Sigma P_{i}\hookrightarrow T_{i}
, that is,

f_{\overline{\beta^{\ast}}}:=\lambda_{e_{\Sigma i}\beta}:T_{s(\beta)}\to T_{i},\quad a\mapsto e_{\Sigma i}\beta a.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in Q_{1}
with
s(\alpha)=i,t(\beta)=i
.
Then define

f_{[\alpha\beta]}:=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}:T_{s(\beta)}\to T_{t(\alpha)},\quad a\mapsto\alpha\beta a.

and

f_{\overline{[\alpha\beta]}}:=0:T_{t(\alpha)}\to T_{s(\beta)}.

Let
\gamma\in Q_{1}
be an arrow not incident to
i
.
Then define

\displaystyle f_{\gamma}

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{\gamma}:T_{s(\gamma)}\to T_{t(\gamma)},\quad a\mapsto\gamma a,

\displaystyle f_{\overline{\gamma}}

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{\overline{\gamma}}:T_{t(\gamma)}\to T_{s(\gamma)},\quad a\mapsto\overline{\gamma}a.

Let
j\in Q_{0}
with
j\neq i
.
Then define

f_{t^{\prime}_{j}}:=\lambda_{t_{j}}:T_{j}\to T_{j},\quad a\mapsto t_{j}a.

It is a morphism of degree
-2
.
Finally,
define
f_{t^{\prime}_{i}}
as the linear morphism of degree
-2
from
T_{i}
to itself given by

f_{t^{\prime}_{i}}((e_{\Sigma i})a_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}e_{\rho}a_{\rho})=-e_{\Sigma i}(t_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{\rho\in Q_{1},s(\rho)=i}\overline{\rho}a_{\rho}).

By
[
31
, Proposition 3.5]
we have the following.

Proposition 11.7
.

The map
f\colon\{e_{j}\mid j\in Q_{0}\}\cup(\widetilde{Q^{\prime}})_{1}\to\operatorname{End}_{\Gamma}(T)

defined above extends to a homomorphism of dg algebras from
\Gamma^{\prime}
to
\operatorname{End}_{\Gamma}(T)
. In this way,
T
becomes a left dg
\Gamma^{\prime}
-module,
and also a dg
\Gamma^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-bimodule.

11.2.
Examples

Example 11.8
.

Let
(Q,W)
be the quiver with potential given as follows:

\textstyle{c_{1}}
\textstyle{b_{2}}
\textstyle{b_{1}}
\textstyle{a_{1}}
\textstyle{a_{2}}
\textstyle{c_{2}}
\textstyle{c_{4}}
\textstyle{a_{4}}
\textstyle{a_{3}}
\textstyle{b_{3}}
\textstyle{b_{4}}

By
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
, the Jacobian algebras
\operatorname{Jac}(Q,W)
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
are derived equivalent.

(1) Consider the cyclic group
G
of order
3
with generator
g
, and define the action of
g
on
(Q,W)

by
i\mapsto i-2
and
a_{i}\mapsto a_{i-2}
(modulo
6
).
Therefore, we have

Ga_{1}=\{a_{1},a_{5},a_{3}\},Ga_{2}=\{a_{2},a_{6},a_{4}\}.

In this case
(Q_{G},W_{G})
is the quiver with potential given as follows:

(2) Next we define the action of
g
on
(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
by

i\mapsto i-2,\ a^{*}_{i}\mapsto a^{*}_{i-2},\ \text{and}\ [a_{i}a_{i+5}]\mapsto[a_{i-2}a_{i+3}]\ (\operatorname{mod}6)

for all

.

Therefore, we have

Ga^{*}_{1}=\{a^{*}_{1},a^{*}_{5},a^{*}_{3}\},Ga^{*}_{2}=\{a^{*}_{2},a^{*}_{6},a^{*}_{4}\}.

In this case
(Q^{\prime}_{G},W^{\prime}_{G})
is the quiver with potential given as follows:

W^{\prime}_{G}=3G[a_{6}a_{1}]G(a^{*}_{1})G(a^{*}_{6})+G([a_{6}a_{1}])^{3}.\end{gathered}

Here the Jacobian algebras

\operatorname{Jac}(Q_{G},W_{G})
and
\operatorname{Jac}(Q^{\prime}_{G},W^{\prime}_{G})
are representation-finite,
selfinjective algebras, and by the main theorem in
[
3
]
, they are derived equivalent
because their derived equivalence types are the same. By Keller-Yang’s result
[
31
]
,
the complete Ginzburg dg algebras
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)
and
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
are derived equivalent as dg algebras.
By using Corollary
10.8
, we will show that

\widehat{\Gamma}(Q,W)/G
and
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})/G
are derived equivalent as dg algebras.
Therefore the complete Ginzburg dg algebras
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q_{G},W_{G})
and
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime}_{G},W^{\prime}_{G})
are derived equivalent as dg algebras by Remark
11.1
.
We set

\Gamma^{(1)}:=\widehat{\Gamma}(\mu_{1}(Q,W)),\Gamma^{(2)}:=\widehat{\Gamma}(\mu_{3}\circ\mu_{1}(Q,W)),\Gamma^{\prime}:=\widehat{\Gamma}(\mu_{5}\circ\mu_{3}\circ\mu_{1}(Q,W))=\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime},W^{\prime})
.
Then Keller–Yang’s theorem (Theorem
11.6
) gives us the following derived equivalences
F_{3},F_{2},F_{1}

defined as
(\text{-})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}T^{(3)}
,
(\text{-})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(2)}}T^{(2)}
,
(\text{-})\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(1)}}T^{(1)}

using the dg bimodules
T^{(3)},T^{(2)},T^{(1)}
constructed as in
Proposition
11.7
, respectively.
These functors send objects as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{F_{3}}\quad{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{(2)})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{F_{2}}\quad{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{(1)})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{F_{1}}\quad{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{5}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P^{(2)}_{5}\to P^{(2)}_{6})

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P^{(1)}_{5}\to P^{(1)}_{6})

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P_{5}\to P_{6})=:T(5)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P^{(2)}_{3}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P^{(1)}_{3}\to P^{(1)}_{4})

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P_{3}\to P_{4})=:T(3)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P^{(2)}_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P^{(1)}_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad(P_{1}\to P_{2})=:T(1)

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P^{(2)}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P^{(1)}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\quad P_{i}=:T(i),(i=2,4,6)\

where
P^{\prime}_{i}=e_{i}\Gamma^{\prime},P^{(2)}_{i}=e_{i}\Gamma^{(2)},P^{(1)}_{i}=e_{i}\Gamma^{(1)}

for all
i\in Q_{0}
.
Then
F:=F_{1}\circ F_{2}\circ F_{3}=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}T^{(3)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(2)}}T^{(2)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(1)}}T^{(1)}
is an equivalence from
{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
to
{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)
.
Here
T^{(3)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(2)}}T^{(2)}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{(1)}}T^{(1)}

is a dg
\Gamma^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-bimodule and
is isomorphic to the direct sum
T
of the indecomposable objects

T(i),(i=1,\dots,6)
as a dg right
\Gamma
-module, by which we identify
these and regard
T
as a dg
\Gamma^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-bimodule.
Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Gamma)
consisting of

T(1),T(2),\cdots,T(6)
.
We show that
{\mathscr{T}}
is a desired tilting subcategory for
\Gamma
.

Now since
g
acts on
P_{i}
by
{}^{g}P_{i}=P_{i-2},(i=1,\dots,6)
by the
G
-action in (1) above, we have

{}^{g}T(i)=T(i-2),(i=1,\dots,6)
.
On the other hand by the
G
-action in (2),
g
acts on
P^{\prime}_{i}
by
{}^{g}P^{\prime}_{i}=P^{\prime}_{i-2},(i=1,\dots,6)
.

We construct a 1-morphism
(F^{\prime},\phi)\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{T}}
that is a
G
-quasi-equivalence.
To this end we have to construct
a quasi-equivalence
F^{\prime}\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{T}}
and a 2-quasi-isomorphism

\phi(a)\colon{\mathscr{T}}(a)\circ F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F^{\prime}\circ a

in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}

for each
a\in G
(see Definition
8.6
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{T}}(a)={}^{a}(\text{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{a}(\text{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi(a)}

(It is trivial that the right square is strictly commutative).
We now define
F^{\prime}
as follows:
First recall the Yoneda embedding

Y\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}(\Gamma^{\prime})
is defined by

Y(i):=\Gamma^{\prime}(\text{-},i)=e_{i}\Gamma^{\prime}
for all
i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
, and

Y(\mu):=\Gamma^{\prime}(\text{-},\mu)
for all
\mu\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
.
Let
\alpha_{M}\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}M\to M
be the usual natural isomorphisim for all
\Gamma^{\prime}
-
\Gamma
-bimodule
M
.
This yields the isomorphism

e_{i}\alpha_{M}\colon e_{i}\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}M\to e_{i}M
for each

i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
that is natural in
i
and in
M
.
Note that the naturality in
i
means that for each
f\colon i\to j
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{e_{i}\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}M}
\textstyle{e_{i}M}
\textstyle{e_{j}\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}M}
\textstyle{e_{j}M.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}\alpha_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}(\text{-},f)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}M}
\scriptstyle{M(\text{-},f)}
\scriptstyle{e_{j}\alpha_{M}}

We then define
F^{\prime}:=F\circ Y\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to\operatorname{per}(\Gamma)\subseteq{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)
,
thus
F^{\prime}(i)=e_{i}\Gamma^{\prime}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}T\xrightarrow{e_{i}\alpha_{T}}T(i)
for all
i\in Q_{0}
, and

F^{\prime}(\mu)=\Gamma^{\prime}(\text{-},\mu)\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}T\cong\lambda_{\mu}\colon T(i)\to T(j)

for all
\mu\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}(i,j)
with
i,j\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
.
Thus we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{F^{\prime}(i)}
\textstyle{T(i)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(j)}
\textstyle{T(j).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{i}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{e_{j}\alpha_{T}}

Next we define a 2-quasi-isomorphism

\phi(a)\colon{}^{a}F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F^{\prime}a
for each
a\in G
.
Let
i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
, and
a\in G
. Then
the isomorphism
e_{i}\alpha_{T}\colon F^{\prime}(i)\to T(i)
yields isomomrphisms

{}^{a}(F^{\prime}(i))\xrightarrow{{}^{a}(e_{i}\alpha_{T})}{}^{a}T(i)=T(ai)
,
and

F^{\prime}(ai)\xrightarrow{e_{ai}\alpha_{T}}T(ai)
.
Thus we have an isomorphism

\phi_{i}(a):=(e_{ai}\alpha_{T})^{-1}\circ{}^{a}(e_{i}\alpha_{T})\colon{}^{a}(F^{\prime}(i))\to F^{\prime}(ai).

We then define
\phi(a):=(\phi_{i}(a))_{i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}\colon{}^{a}F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F^{\prime}a
for all
a\in G
and

\phi:=(\phi(a))_{a\in G}
.

Claim 1
.

The pair
(F^{\prime},\phi)
is a
1
-morphism

\Gamma^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{T}}

(see Definition
2.13
)
.

Indeed, because
F^{\prime}(i)
is clearly a dg-functor, it suffices to show that

\phi(a)
is a 2-morphism in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
for each
a\in G
.
Namely, we have to show the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{{}^{a}F^{\prime}(u)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(au)}
\textstyle{{}^{a}F^{\prime}(v)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(av)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{u}(a)}
\scriptstyle{{}^{a}F^{\prime}(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{v}(a)}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(a\mu)}

for all
\mu\colon u\to v
in
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
a\in G
.
It suffices to show the commutativity of this
only for
a=g
and for all
\mu\in\widetilde{Q^{\prime}}_{1}
.
Therefore finally we have only to show the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{{}^{g}F^{\prime}(u)}
\textstyle{{}^{g}T(u)}
\textstyle{T(gu)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(gu)}
\textstyle{{}^{g}F^{\prime}(v)}
\textstyle{{}^{g}T(v)}
\textstyle{T(gv)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(gv)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{g}(e_{u}\alpha_{T})}
\scriptstyle{e_{a_{u}}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{g}F^{\prime}(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{e_{a_{v}}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{e_{a_{v}}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(g\mu)}
\scriptstyle{{}^{g}T(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{T(g\mu)}

(11.34)

for all
\mu\in\widetilde{Q^{\prime}}_{1}
.
We check this only for three cases below.
The remaining cases are checked similarly, and is left to the reader.

Now the quivers of
\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{(2)},\Gamma^{(1)},\Gamma
are given as follows:

Case 1.

\mu=a^{*}_{i}\in\widetilde{Q^{\prime}}
for some
i=1,\dots,6
, say
i=1
.
Then up to Yoneda embeddings (for the first three correspondences) we have

a^{*}_{1}\overset{F_{3}}{\mapsto}a^{*}_{1}\overset{F_{2}}{\mapsto}a^{*}_{1}\overset{F_{1}}{\mapsto}f_{a^{*}_{1}}\overset{{}^{g}(\text{-})}{\mapsto}f_{a^{*}_{5}}
.
Since we have commutative diagrams

and hence (

) is verified in this case.

Case 2.

\mu=\overline{a^{*}_{i}}\in\widetilde{Q^{\prime}}
for some
i=1,\dots,6
, say
i=1
.
Then up to Yoneda embeddings (for the first three correspondences) we have

\overline{a^{*}_{1}}\overset{F_{3}}{\mapsto}\overline{a^{*}_{1}}\overset{F_{2}}{\mapsto}\overline{a^{*}_{1}}\overset{F_{1}}{\mapsto}f_{\overline{a^{*}_{1}}}\overset{{}^{g}(\text{-})}{\mapsto}f_{\overline{a^{*}_{5}}}
.
Since we have commutative diagrams

We check the conditions (a) and (b) in Definition
2.13
.

Verifications of (a):

This is equivalent to the equation that
\phi(1)=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{F^{\prime}}
, which
follows from the construction of
\phi
and the fact that both
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
{\mathscr{T}}

have strict
G
-actions.

Verification of (b):

This condition is equivalent to saying that the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{{}^{b}({}^{a}(F^{\prime}(i)))}
\textstyle{{}^{b}((F^{\prime}(ai)))}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(bai)}
\scriptstyle{{}^{b}(\phi_{i}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{(ai)}(b)}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{i}(ba)}

(11.35)

for all
a,b\in G
and
i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
.
By definition of
\phi_{i}(a)
, the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{{}^{a}(F^{\prime}(i))}
\textstyle{{}^{a}T(i)}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(ai)}
\textstyle{T(ai).}
\scriptstyle{{}^{a}(e_{i}\alpha_{T})}
\scriptstyle{e_{ai}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{i}(a)}

This yields the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{}^{b}({}^{a}(F^{\prime}(i)))}
\textstyle{{}^{b}({}^{a}(T(i)))}
\textstyle{{}^{ba}(F^{\prime}(i))}
\textstyle{{}^{b}(F^{\prime}(ai))}
\textstyle{{}^{b}(T(ai))}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(bai)}
\textstyle{T(b(ai))}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(bai),}
\scriptstyle{{}^{b}(^{a}(e_{i}\alpha_{T}))}
\scriptstyle{{}^{b}(e_{ai}\alpha_{T})}
\scriptstyle{{}^{b}(\phi_{i}(a))}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{ai}(b)}
\scriptstyle{e_{b(ai)}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{{}^{ba}(e_{i}\alpha_{T})}
\scriptstyle{e_{bai}\alpha_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{i}(ba)}

which shows the commutativity of the diagram (
11.35
).

It remains to show that
(F^{\prime},\phi)
is a quasi-equivalence.
Namely we have to show the following claims:

Claim 2
.

F^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, and hence a quasi-equivalence.

Indeed, we regard
\Gamma^{\prime}
as a dg category following Remark
11.4
.
For each
i\in Q_{0}
, we have
F^{\prime}(i)=T(i)
.
Hence
F^{\prime}
is bijective on objects.
Moreover, for each
i,j\in Q_{0}
, we have
a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma^{\prime})(\Gamma^{\prime}(\operatorname{-},i),\Gamma^{\prime}(\operatorname{-},j))}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}(\Gamma)(F(i),F(j))}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}(i,j)}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{T}}(F^{\prime}(i),F^{\prime}(j)),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}

where
Y
and
F
above are bijective.
Hence
F^{\prime}
above is bijective.

Claim 3
.

\phi(a)
is a 2-quasi-isomorphism for all
a\in G
, i.e.,

{\mathscr{T}}(\text{-},\phi_{i}(a))\colon{\mathscr{T}}(\text{-},{}^{a}F^{\prime}(i))\to{\mathscr{T}}(\text{-},F^{\prime}(ai))
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{T}})

for all
a\in G
and
i\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}
.

Indeed,
by construction
\phi_{i}(a)\colon{}^{a}F^{\prime}(i)\to F^{\prime}(ai)
is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Therefore
{\mathscr{T}}(\text{-},\phi_{i}(a))
is an isomorpism in
\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}}({\mathscr{T}})
, and thus
it is a quasi-isomorphism.

As a consequence,

\widehat{\Gamma}(Q_{G},W_{G})
and
\widehat{\Gamma}(Q^{\prime}_{G},W^{\prime}_{G})
are derived equivalent.
Note that the quivers with potentials
(Q_{G},W_{G})
and
(Q^{\prime}_{G},W^{\prime}_{G})

are not mutated from each other in this case.
Therefore we cannot apply
[
31
, Theorem 3.2]
by
Keller-Yang
to have this derived equivalence.

To give an example of the case that the category
I
is not a group, we need to give how to compute the Grothendieck construction of a functor

X\colon I\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathbf{dgCat}
at least.
This will be done in the forthcoming paper, which will include such an example.

A.
Quasi-equivalence morphisms and derived equivalences

The following statement is stated in
[
30
]
without a proof
in a remark after
[
30
, Lemma 3.10]
.
For completeness, we give a proof of it in this appendix.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
E:{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{B}}
be a quasi-equivalence between dg categories
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
. Then

\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}\overline{E}\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})

is an equivalence of triangulated categories,
where
\overline{E}
is the
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule

\overline{E}:={}_{E}{\mathscr{B}}
.
In particular,
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
are derived equivalent.

For the proof we prepare the following three lemmas.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
{\mathscr{D}}
and
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
be triangulated categories, and

F:{\mathscr{D}}\to{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
and
G:{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}\to{\mathscr{D}}
triangle functors.
Assume that the following conditions are satisfied

(1)

F
is fully faithful,

(2)

G
is a right adjoint to
F
, and

(3)

G(X)=0
implies
X=0
for all objects
X
of
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
.

Then
F
is an equivalence.

Proof.

We denote the unit and the counit of the adjoint by
\eta:1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{\mathscr{D}}\Rightarrow G\circ F
and by
\varepsilon:F\circ G\Rightarrow 1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}}
, respectively.
Let
D\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
, and take a distinguished triangle

FG(D)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{D}}D\to Y\to FG(D)[1]

in
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}
.
Apply the functor
G
to get

GFG(D)\xrightarrow{G(\varepsilon_{D})}G(D)\to G(Y)\to G(D)[1].

Since
F
is fully faithful,
\eta:1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}\Rightarrow G\circ F
is an isomorphism.
In particular,
\eta_{G(D)}
is an isomorphism.
Then the equality
G(\varepsilon_{D})\eta_{G(D)}=1\kern-2.5pt{\text{{\rm l}}}_{G(D)}
yields
a commutative diagram with triangle rows:

Thus
G(Y)=0
. Therefore,
Y=0
and
FG(D)\cong D
.
Hence
F
is an equivalence.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories,
and
N
a dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule.
Assume that

(1)

the dg module
{}_{A}N
is compact in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
for all
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
,

(2)

The canonical morphism
\alpha_{Y,Z,k}\colon H^{k}({\mathscr{A}}(Y,Z))\to\operatorname{Hom}_{{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})}({}_{Y}N,{}_{Z}N[k])
is an isomorphism for all
Y,Z\in{\mathscr{A}}
and for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Then
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N
is fully faithful.

Proof.

We know that
(\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N,\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,\operatorname{-}))
is an adjoint pair, say with the usual unit
\eta
. Therefore to show that
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N
is fully faithful, it suffices to show the following.

Claim
.

For each
M\in{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
,

\eta_{M}:M\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,M\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N)

is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.

To show this, let
{\mathscr{C}}
be the full subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
formed by those objects
M
such that
\eta_{M}
is an isomorphism.
To show the claim we have only to show that
{\mathscr{C}}={\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
As is easily seen
{\mathscr{C}}
is a triangulated subcategory of
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
Therefore it suffices to show the following two facts:

(i)

{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}\in{\mathscr{C}}
for all
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
; and

(ii)

{\mathscr{C}}
is closed under small coproducts.

(i) Let
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
. We show that
{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}\in{\mathscr{C}}
, namely that

\eta_{{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}}:{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N)\cong\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,{}_{A}N)

is an isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
.
It suffices to show that

\eta_{{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}}:{}_{A}{\mathscr{A}}\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,{}_{A}N)

is a quasi-isomorphism.
For each
A^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{A}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
we have the following commutative diagram:

\hbox to324.42pt{\vbox to59.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 162.20848pt\lower-27.5228pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where (a) is an isomorphism because
\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathscr{A}}N
is a left adjoint
and preserves small coproducts, and (b) is an isomorphism by the assumption (1).
Thus

\eta_{\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}}:\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}\to\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N)

is an isomorphism,
and hence we have

\bigoplus_{i\in I}M_{i}\in{\mathscr{C}}
.
As a consequence,
{\mathscr{C}}
is closed under small coproducts.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
be dg categories and

E\colon{\mathscr{A}}\to{\mathscr{B}}
a quasi-equivalence.
Then
for each right
{\mathscr{B}}
-module
M
the following holds:

\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{E}{\mathscr{B}},M)=0\text{ implies }M=0.

Proof.

Let
M
be a
{\mathscr{B}}
-module, and assume that
\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{E}{\mathscr{B}},M)=0
.
Take any
B\in{\mathscr{B}}
.
It is enough to show that
M(B)=0
.
Now, since
H^{0}(E):H^{0}({\mathscr{A}})\to H^{0}({\mathscr{B}})
is an equivalence (the condition (2) in Definition
7.32
), there exists an object
A\in{\mathscr{A}}
, such that
E(A)=H^{0}(E)(A)\cong B
in
H^{0}({\mathscr{B}})
.
Then by the functor

H^{0}({\mathscr{B}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}}),X\mapsto{}_{X}{\mathscr{B}}

we have

{}_{E(A)}{\mathscr{B}}\cong{}_{B}{\mathscr{B}}
in
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
.
Hence by the dg Yoneda lemma we have

M(B)\cong\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{B}{\mathscr{B}},M)\cong\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}({}_{E(A)}{\mathscr{B}},M)=0,

as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
A.1

Define a dg
{\mathscr{A}}
-
{\mathscr{B}}
-bimodule
N
by
N:={}_{E}{\mathscr{B}}
.
Then
N
satisfies the condition (1) in Lemma
A.3
,
and by the assumption (in particular, by the condition (1) in Definition
7.32
)
N
also satisfies the condition (2) in Lemma
A.3
.
Therefore
F:=\operatorname{-}\overset{\mathbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathscr{A}}}N\colon{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})\to{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
is fully faithful by Lemma
A.3
.
Moreover
G:=\mathbf{R}\!\operatorname{{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{dg}}}_{{\mathscr{B}}}(N,\operatorname{-})
is a right adjoint to
F
and satisfies the condition (3) in Lemma
A.2

by the assumption and Lemma
A.4
.
Hence
F
is an equivalence between
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{A}})
and
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathscr{B}})
by Lemma
A.2
.
∎
